As a convention, the following constraints are imposed on the coefficients of such decompositions: coefficients of frame elements involving products of the same Majorana operators must have equal magnitude, and coefficients associated with zero operators in the module frame are set to zero. These otherwise non-unique decompositions then yield completely anti-symmetric rank-
m
tensors.

Next, we recall the definitions of projections onto the modules, module overlaps (i.e., inner products in the module) and module purities (i.e., norms in the module). These definitions are formulated with respect to both the module basis and frame.

Module projection.

Consider the space of linear operators
\mathcal{B}
acting on
\mathbb{C}^{d}
with
d=2^{n}
, which can be decomposed into the modules
\mathcal{B}_{m}
as in Eq. (
57
).
Given an operator
A\in\mathcal{B}
, the orthogonal projection onto the modules is denoted as
A_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}
, or more concisely,
A_{m}
.
The projection onto the module expanded using the module basis or module frame is given by

A_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathcal{V}\subseteq[2n]\\
\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert=m\end{subarray}}a_{\mathcal{V}}C_{\mathcal{V}}\qquad\text{with}\qquad a_{\mathcal{V}}=\Braket{C_{\mathcal{V}},A}={\rm Tr}\left[C_{\mathcal{V}}A\right]\,,

(60)

and

A_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}=\sum_{\bm{\nu}\in[2n]^{m}}a_{\bm{\nu}}C_{\bm{\nu}}\qquad\text{with}\qquad a_{\bm{\nu}}=\Braket{C_{\bm{\nu}},A}={\rm Tr}\left[C_{\bm{\nu}}A\right]\,,

(61)

respectively,
where
\Braket{\cdot,\cdot}
denotes the
hs
inner product. Recall that
C_{\mathcal{V}}
with the index set
\mathcal{V}
relates to the product of Majorana operators in the unique ascending order
\nu_{1}<\cdots<\nu_{m}\in\mathcal{V}
. In contrast, the index vector
\bm{\nu}
determines the order of the Majorana operators explicitly.
Note that obtaining the coefficients for the frame as
a_{\bm{\nu}}={\rm Tr}\left[C_{\bm{\nu}}A\right]
implicitly enforces that all coefficients corresponding to index vectors
\bm{\nu}
with non-distinct entries are zero.
Importantly, the sums in Eq. (
60
) and Eq. (
61
) run over
\binom{2n}{m}
and
(2n)^{m}
terms, respectively. Note as well that the coefficients with respect to both module basis and frame are real if and only if the decomposed or projected operator is Hermitian.

The coefficients obtained by projections via the module basis relate to the coefficients of the module frame as

a_{\bm{\nu}}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\sqrt{m!}}\,\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\,a_{\left\{\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{m}\right\}}&\text{if }\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{m}\text{ distinct}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\,,\end{cases}

(62)

where
\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\in\{-1,1\}
denotes the sign of the permutation
\pi
of the index vector
\bm{\nu}=\pi(\bm{\nu}^{\prime})
, with respect to the reference index vector
\bm{\nu}^{\prime}
with the same but ordered components
\nu_{1}^{\prime}<\nu_{2}^{\prime}<\cdots<\nu_{m}^{\prime}
.
Equivalently, the generalized (anti-symmetric) Kronecker delta
\delta
yields a concise definition by combining both cases of distinct and non-distinct indices as

a_{\bm{\nu}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m!}}a_{\left\{\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots,\nu_{m}\right\}}\delta_{\nu_{1}\phantom{{}^{\prime}}\nu_{2}\ldots\nu_{m}}^{\nu^{\prime}_{1}\nu^{\prime}_{2}\dots\nu^{\prime}_{m}}\,,

(63)

where
\bm{\nu}^{\prime}
is the ordered reference
\nu_{1}^{\prime}<\nu_{2}^{\prime}<\cdots<\nu_{{m}}^{\prime}
.
The generalized Kronecker delta is a rank-
m
tensor and evaluates its components
\delta_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}}^{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{m}}
as
1
(
-1
) if the index values are distinct integers and
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
is an even (odd) permutation of
\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{m}
, and zero otherwise.
Formally, generalized Kronecker delta can be defined in terms of the symmetric group
S_{m}
of degree
m
or an
m\times m
determinant and reads as

\delta_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}}^{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{m}}=\sum_{\pi\in S_{m}}\operatorname{sign}(\pi)\delta_{\alpha_{\sigma(1)}}^{\beta_{1}}\cdots\delta_{\alpha_{\sigma(m)}}^{\beta_{m}}=\begin{vmatrix}\delta^{\beta_{1}}_{\alpha_{1}}&\dots&\delta^{\beta_{1}}_{\alpha_{m}}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\delta^{\beta_{m}}_{\alpha_{1}}&\dots&\delta^{\beta_{m}}_{\alpha_{m}}\end{vmatrix}\,,

(64)

reducing to the standard (two-index) Kronecker delta, which is
1
if
\alpha=\beta
and otherwise zero, denoted as
\delta_{\alpha}^{\beta}=\delta_{\alpha,\beta}
.

Vice versa, the module frame coefficients determine the coefficients of projections in the module basis as

a_{\mathcal{V}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m!}}\sum_{\pi\in S_{m}}\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)a_{\pi(\mathcal{V})}\,,

(65)

where
\pi\in S_{m}
denotes the possible permutations (on
m
indices) such that
\pi(\mathcal{V})
provides an index vector of the index set
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,2n\}
(
\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert=m
) in a certain fixed order. In common special cases, e.g., when the module frame coefficients were obtained by projecting into the module using all frame operators, the single frame coefficient with the index vector
\bm{\nu}^{\prime}
corresponding to ordered indices
\nu_{1}^{\prime}<\nu_{2}^{\prime}<\cdots<\nu_{m}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
determines the basis coefficient

a_{\mathcal{V}}=\sqrt{m!}\,a_{\bm{\nu}^{\prime}}\,.

(66)

Module overlap (module inner product).
The module overlap between two operators
A,B\in\mathcal{B}
is defined as the HS inner products of their module projections, i.e.,

\Braket{A,B}_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}=\Braket{A_{m},B_{m}}={\rm Tr}\left[A_{m}^{\dagger}B_{m}\right]\,,

(67)

where
\Braket{\cdot,\cdot}
denotes the
hs
inner product.
Utilizing the decompositions of the projected operators into the orthonormal module basis and frame, given in Eqs. (
60
) and (
61
) respectively, the module overlaps become

\Braket{A,B}_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}=\Braket{\bm{a},\bm{b}}=\sum_{\mathcal{V}}a_{\mathcal{V}}^{*}b_{\mathcal{V}}=\sum_{\bm{\nu}}a_{\bm{\nu}}^{*}b_{\bm{\nu}}\,,

(68)

where
\Braket{\cdot,\cdot}
denotes the standard scalar product on complex vectors. Notice that the sum
\sum_{\bm{\nu}}\bm{a}_{\bm{\nu}}^{*}b_{\bm{\nu}}
corresponds to the full contraction of the coefficient tensors
\bm{a}_{\bm{\nu}}
and
\bm{b}_{\bm{\nu}}
.

Equivalently, the module overlap can be expressed as

\Braket{A,B}_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}=\sum_{\mathcal{V}}{\rm Tr}\left[C_{\mathcal{V}}^{\otimes 2}\left(A^{\dagger}\otimes B\right)\right]={\rm Tr}\left[\left(\sum_{\mathcal{V}}C_{\mathcal{V}}^{\otimes 2}\right)\left(A^{\dagger}\otimes B\right)\right]=\Braket{\sum_{\mathcal{V}}C_{\mathcal{V}}^{\otimes 2},\;A^{\dagger}\otimes B}_{\!\mathrm{HS}}.

(69)

Analogously, in terms of the module frame operators,

\Braket{A,B}_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}=\sum_{\bm{\nu}}{\rm Tr}\left[C_{\bm{\nu}}^{\otimes 2}\left(A^{\dagger}\otimes B\right)\right]={\rm Tr}\left[\left(\sum_{\bm{\nu}}C_{\bm{\nu}}^{\otimes 2}\right)\left(A^{\dagger}\otimes B\right)\right]=\Braket{\sum_{\bm{\nu}}C_{\bm{\nu}}^{\otimes 2},\;A^{\dagger}\otimes B}_{\!\mathrm{HS}}.

(70)

Note that, despite their direct connection to state overlaps, module overlaps can attain negative values. For instance, consider the two single-qubit states
\rho_{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(I\pm X\right)
, which are orthogonal.
However, the projection onto
\mathcal{B}_{1}
does not preserve orthogonality and even results in a negative module overlap, as
\Braket{\rho_{+},\rho_{-}}_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}=-\tfrac{1}{2}
.

Module purities (module norm).
The module purity of an operator
A\in\mathcal{B}
is defined as the squared HS inner norm of its module projection, i.e.,

\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}^{2}=\left\lVert A_{m}\right\rVert^{2}=\Braket{A,A}_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}={\rm Tr}\left[A_{m}^{\dagger}A_{m}\right]\,,

(71)

where
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
denotes the
hs
norm.
Alternative expressions for the module purity follow immediately from Eqs. (
68
), (
69
) and (
70
). Notably, since

\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}^{2}=\lVert\bm{a}\lVert^{2}=\sum_{\mathcal{V}}\lvert a_{\mathcal{V}}\rvert^{2}=\sum_{\bm{\nu}}\lvert a_{\bm{\nu}}\rvert^{2},

(72)

the module frame is a Parseval frame, i.e., the norm of the projection coefficients in the module frame matches the norm of the operator in the module subspace. This implies that the module purity is upper bounded for quantum states as

\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}^{2}\leq 1
.

Furthermore, we can apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to obtain an upper bound for the module overlap,

\left|\Braket{A,B}_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}\right|\leq\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}^{2}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}^{2}\,.

(73)

In particular, for quantum states
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
, the absolute value of their module overlap is bounded as
\Braket{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}\leq d_{m}/d
.

A.4
The Haar measure and twirl superoperator

The normalized Haar measure
\mu_{\mathrm{H}}
on a semisimple Lie group
\operatorname{\mathbb{G}}
is uniquely defined by its left-and-right-invariance property,

\int_{\operatorname{\mathbb{G}}}f(U)d\mu_{\mathrm{H}}(U)=\int_{\operatorname{\mathbb{G}}}f(VU)d\mu_{\mathrm{H}}(U)=\int_{\operatorname{\mathbb{G}}}f(UV)d\mu_{\mathrm{H}}(U)\,,

(74)

for any integrable function
f(U)
and
V\in\operatorname{\mathbb{G}}
.
For the sake of brevity, we usually omit the label
\mu_{\mathrm{H}}
.

The
t
-th moment superoperator
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{\mathbb{G}}}^{(t)}(\cdot)
induced by the adjoint action under the Haar measure, referred to as the twirl, is given by
Mele 2024

\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{\mathbb{G}}}^{(t)}(\cdot)=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim_{\mu_{\rm H}}\operatorname{\mathbb{G}}}\left[U^{\otimes t}(\cdot)(U^{\dagger})^{\otimes t}\right].

(75)

This linear superoperator is a map between linear operators on
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes t}
, and can be vectorized to yield the (vectorized) moment operator
M_{\operatorname{\mathbb{G}}}^{(t)}
, as

M_{\operatorname{\mathbb{G}}}^{(t)}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim_{\mu_{\rm H}}\operatorname{\mathbb{G}}}\!\left[U^{\otimes t}\otimes(U^{*})^{\otimes t}\right].

(76)

Th moment operator
M_{\operatorname{\mathbb{G}}}^{(t)}
acts on vectors of dimension
{2^{2nt}}
, which are the vectorizations of linear operators acting on
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes t}
.

Supp. Info. B
Theoretical framework for matchgate Gaussian processes

In this section, we develop the framework for analyzing the emergence of Gaussian processes under random matchgate unitaries. In particular, we provide the proof for Theorem
1
.

We begin by introducing the random variables
5
5

5

Note the common convention of using upper-case letters to denote random variables, which should not be confused with upper-case letters used to denote operators or matrices. The random variables studied here have scalar-valued realizations.

X_{i}={\rm Tr}\left[U\rho_{i}U^{\dagger}O\right]\,,

(77)

with
U
sampled from the Haar measure on
\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)

Braccia et al. 2025
.
Our goal is to analyze the distributions of the expectation values in Eq. (
77
) over quantum states
\{\rho_{i}\}_{i}
that have been evolved under some free-fermionic transformation
U
, and determine conditions for which they converge to Gaussian processes under the prior that
U
had been uniformly sampled from
\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)
, i.e. according to the latter’s Haar measure.
Hence, we are interested in computing the
k
-th moments

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{k}{\rm Tr}\left[U\rho_{i_{j}}U^{\dagger}O\right]\right]\,,

(78)

corresponding to the index multiset
\{i_{j}\}_{j=1}^{k}
identifying the random variables
X_{i_{j}}
(and states
\rho_{i_{j}}
). We will consider both the univariate (single input state) and multivariate (multiple input states) cases.
For univariate distributions, where a single random variable
X
is analyzed, the moment of order
k
reduces to

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[X^{k}\right]
. For multivariate distributions of
N
random variables or, equivalently,
N
-dimensional random vectors,
we will collect the random variables in a length-
N
vector
\left(X_{1},X_{2},\dots,X_{N}\right)^{\top}
.

We restrict our derivations to an observable
O
from a specific module
\mathcal{B}_{m}
(
m\neq 0,2n
) , i.e., a linear combination of products of
m
distinct Majorana operators. Consequently, the primary mathematical objects in the derivation of this theoretical framework are the module frame projection coefficients for the states
\rho_{i}
and the observable
O
(as defined in Eq. (
61
)). These coefficients are organized into rank-
m
tensors, denoted by
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i)}
and
\bm{O}
, respectively. Each of the
m
tensor indices, collected in the index vector
\bm{\nu}
, corresponds to a single Majorana index within the frame operator product
C_{\bm{\nu}}
. For the cases of tensor rank
m=2
and
m=1
, these coefficient tensors simplify to matrices (denoted
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i)}
and
O
) and vectors (denoted
\bm{\rho}^{(i)}
and
\bm{o}
), respectively. Further details regarding notation of tensors and their form variants are provided in Supp. Info.
A.1
.

The derivation is split into four parts:

1.

We derive general expressions for the moments in Eq. (
78
), connecting them to the standard representation of the special orthogonal group
\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
.
Importantly, we either obtain vanishing moments or recover the (vectorized)
mk/2
-th moment operator with respect to
\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
→ Supp. Info.
B.1
.

2.

We employ asymptotic Weingarten calculus
Mele 2024
;
García-Martín et al. 2025
;
García-Martín et al. 2025
to analyze the moment operator in terms of Brauer algebras and in the asymptotic large-
n
limit → Supp. Info.
B.2
.

3.

We establish formal conditions on the observable
O
and input states
\rho_{i}
under which the random variables
X_{i}
converge to a Gaussian process (Theorem
1
) or not (Theorem
2
) → Supp. Info.
B.3
.

4.

We provide equivalent and sufficient formulations of the conditions established in the previous step, as well as general implications and simplifications of these conditions for certain modules
\mathcal{B}_{m}
→ Supp. Info.
B.4
.

Supplemental Info.
C
identifies concrete families of states and observables that obey or do not obey Gaussian processes under Haar-random matchgate transformations.

B.1
Vectorization reveals the moment operator of the special orthogonal group

We will now connect the moments in Eq. (
78
) to the standard representation of
\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
, as stated in the following useful lemma. This will allow us to leverage well-known Weingarten-calculus results from the special orthogonal group in the next subsections.

Lemma B.1
.

Consider an
observable
O\in\mathcal{B}_{m}
and a set of input states
\left\{\rho_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{N}
. The
k
-th moments of the joint distribution of random variables
X_{i}={\rm Tr}[U\rho_{i}U^{\dagger}O]
are given by

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]=\langle\!\bra{\bm{O}}^{\otimes k}\mathbb{E}_{Q\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}\left[Q^{\otimes mk}\right]\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\ket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle\,,

(79)

where
\ket{\bm{O}}\!\rangle=\sum_{\bm{\mu}}o_{\bm{\mu}}\Ket{\bm{\mu}}
and
\ket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle=\sum_{\bm{\alpha}}\rho^{(i_{j})}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ket{\bm{\alpha}}
are the vectorization (in
\mathbb{R}^{2nm}
) of the tensors
\bm{O}
and
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}
containing the module frame projection coefficients
o_{\bm{\mu}}
and
\rho^{(i_{j})}_{\bm{\alpha}}
, respectively.

Proof.

Employing the frame decomposition of the observable

O=O_{m}=\sum_{\bm{\mu}}o_{\bm{\mu}}C_{\bm{\mu}}
in Eq. (
78
)
yields

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in[2n]^{m}}o_{\bm{\mu}}{\rm Tr}\left[\rho_{i_{j}}UC_{\bm{\mu}}U^{\dagger}\right]\right].

(80)

The matchgate representation, as defined in Eq. (
56
), now directly relates the above expression to the moments over the special orthogonal group
\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
, as follows

UC_{\mu_{1}}C_{\mu_{2}}\cdots C_{\mu_{m}}U^{\dagger}=UC_{\mu_{1}}U^{\dagger}UC_{\mu_{2}}^{\dagger}U\cdots C_{\mu_{m}}U^{\dagger}=\left(\sum_{\alpha_{1}=1}^{2n}q_{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}C_{\alpha_{1}}\right)\left(\sum_{\alpha_{2}=1}^{2n}q_{\mu_{2}\alpha_{2}}C_{\alpha_{2}}\right)\cdots\left(\sum_{\alpha_{m}=1}^{2n}q_{\mu_{m}\alpha_{m}}C_{\alpha_{m}}\right)\,,

(81)

which implies

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{Q\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\left(\sum_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\alpha}\in[2n]^{m}}o_{\bm{\mu}}\,q_{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}\cdots q_{\mu_{m}\alpha_{m}}{\rm Tr}\left[\rho_{i_{j}}C_{\bm{\alpha}}\right]\right)\right]\,.

(82)

The expectation values
{\rm Tr}\left[\rho_{i_{j}}C_{\bm{\alpha}}\right]

are the coefficients in the projection of the state
\rho_{i_{j}}
onto the module
\mathcal{B}_{m}
via the module frame (see Eq. (
61
)), which we denote
\rho_{\bm{\alpha}}^{(i_{j})}={\rm Tr}\left[\rho_{i_{j}}C_{\bm{\alpha}}\right]
. We collect them in a (real) anti-symmetric rank-
m
tensor
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}
. Analogously, the coefficients
o_{\bm{\mu}}
of the observable
O
are collected in another (real) anti-symmetric rank-
m
tensor
\bm{O}
.

Next, we notice that
q_{\mu_{l}\alpha_{l}}=\Braket{\mu_{l}|Q|\alpha_{l}}
, where
|\mu_{l}\rangle
and
|\alpha_{l}\rangle
are canonical basis vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
on which the fundamental representation of
\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
acts
6
6

6

It is important to not confuse the basis vectors
\ket{\mu_{l}}
and
\ket{\alpha_{l}}
corresponding to
\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
with computational (qubit) basis states, as the basis is not binary
\{\ket{0},\ket{1}\}
but composed of
2n
basis elements
\{C_{1},\ldots,C_{2n}\}
.
. This allows us to write

\displaystyle q_{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}\cdots q_{\mu_{m}\alpha_{m}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{l=1}^{m}q_{\mu_{l}\alpha_{l}}=\prod_{l=1}^{m}\Braket{\mu_{l}|Q|\alpha_{l}}

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}\left[\left(\bigotimes_{l=1}^{m}\bra{\mu_{l}}\right)Q^{\otimes m}\left(\bigotimes_{l=1}^{m}\ket{\alpha_{l}}\right)\right]

(83)

\displaystyle=\Braket{\bm{\mu}|Q^{\otimes m}|\bm{\alpha}}\,,

(84)

where Eq. (
83
) can be seen as a consequence of the trace trick
a={\rm Tr}[a]
for any scalar
a
, and the identity
{\rm Tr}[A\otimes B]={\rm Tr}[A]{\rm Tr}[B]
. Furthermore, we introduced the notation
\ket{\bm{\mu}}=\bigotimes_{l=1}^{m}\ket{\mu_{l}}
(and analogously for
\ket{\bm{\alpha}}
).
Plugging Eq. (
84
) into (
82
) leads to

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{Q\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\left(\sum_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\alpha}\in[2n]^{m}}o_{\bm{\mu}}\,q_{\mu_{1}\alpha_{1}}\cdots q_{\mu_{m}\alpha_{m}}\rho^{(i_{j})}_{\bm{\alpha}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{Q\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\left(\sum_{\bm{\mu},\bm{\alpha}\in[2n]^{m}}o_{\bm{\mu}}\Braket{\bm{\mu}|Q^{\otimes m}|\bm{\alpha}}\rho^{(i_{j})}_{\bm{\alpha}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{Q\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{k}\left(\left(\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in[2n]^{m}}o_{\bm{\mu}}\bra{\bm{\mu}}\right)Q^{\otimes m}\left(\sum_{\bm{\alpha}\in[2n]^{m}}\rho^{(i_{j})}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ket{\bm{\alpha}}\right)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{Q\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}\left[\left(\sum_{\bm{\mu}\in[2n]^{m}}o_{\bm{\mu}}\bra{\bm{\mu}}\right)^{\otimes k}Q^{\otimes mk}\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{\bm{\alpha}\in[2n]^{m}}\rho^{(i_{j})}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ket{\bm{\alpha}}\right)\right]\,.

(85)

Finally, using the notation
\ket{\bm{O}}\!\rangle=\sum_{\bm{\mu}}o_{\bm{\mu}}\Ket{\bm{\mu}}
and
\ket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle=\sum_{\bm{\alpha}}\rho^{(i_{j})}_{\bm{\alpha}}\ket{\bm{\alpha}}
for the vectorization of the tensors
\bm{O}
and
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}
, respectively, we end up with

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{Q\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}\left[\langle\!\bra{\bm{O}}^{\otimes k}Q^{\otimes mk}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\ket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle\right]=\ \langle\!\bra{\bm{O}}^{\otimes k}E_{Q\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}\left[Q^{\otimes mk}\right]\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\ket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle\,.

∎

In Supp. Fig.
B.1
we present a tensor-network diagram visualization of Eq. (
79
), which will be very useful for our purposes.

Supplemental Figure B.1:
Tensor network diagram for moment expression.
Depiction in vectorized form with Haar-random
Q\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
as in Eq. (
79
), which yields the moment operator as in Eqs. (
87
) and (
88
).

Next, we show that the moments in Eq. (
79
) either vanish (when
mk
is odd) or else correspond to the action of the (vectorized)
\frac{mk}{2}
-th moment operator in
\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
as defined in Eq.(
76
).

Lemma B.2
.

The
k
-th moments (
78
) either a) vanish

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]=0\qquad\text{{if $mk$ is odd}}\,,

(86)

or b) can be expressed through the (vectorized)
mk/2
-th twirl operator in
\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
w.r.t. the Haar measure,

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]=\langle\!\bra{\bm{O}}^{\otimes k}M_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}^{(mk/2)}\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\ket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle\qquad\text{{if $mk$ is even}}\,,

(87)

where

M_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}^{(mk/2)}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{Q\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}\!\left[Q^{\otimes mk/2}\otimes Q^{\otimes mk/2}\right].

(88)

Proof.

First, if both the moment
k
and the number of Majoranas
m
are odd, then
mk
is odd, and this immediately yields vanishing moments,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]

\displaystyle=\langle\!\bra{\bm{O}}^{\otimes k}\left(\int_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}Q^{\otimes mk}d\mu_{H}(Q)\right)\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\ket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\!\bra{\bm{O}}^{\otimes k}\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}Q^{\otimes mk}d\mu_{H}(Q)+\int_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}Q^{\otimes mk}d\mu_{H}(Q)\right)\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\ket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\!\bra{\bm{O}}^{\otimes k}\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}Q^{\otimes mk}d\mu_{H}(Q)+\int_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}(-IQ)^{\otimes mk}d\mu_{H}(Q)\right)\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\ket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle

(89)

\displaystyle=\langle\!\bra{\bm{O}}^{\otimes k}\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}Q^{\otimes mk}d\mu_{H}(Q)+\int_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}(-1)^{mk}Q^{\otimes mk}d\mu_{H}(Q)\right)\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\ket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\!\bra{\bm{O}}^{\otimes k}\frac{1}{2}\left(\int_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}Q^{\otimes mk}d\mu_{H}(Q)-\int_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}Q^{\otimes mk}d\mu_{H}(Q)\right)\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\ket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle

(90)

\displaystyle=0\,.

(91)

Here, Eq. (
89
) utilizes the left-invariance of the Haar measure
\mu_{H}
(see Eq. (
74
)) under left multiplication by an element of the group, which is
-I\in\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
in this case. In Eq. (
90
), we used that
mk
is odd, so that
(-1)^{mk}=-1
. This proves case
a)
.

For case
b)
, we notice that if at least the moment
k
or the number of Majoranas
m
is even,
mk
is even, and the expectation in Eq. (
79
) can be re-written as

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{Q\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}\!\left[Q^{\otimes mk}\right]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{Q\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}\!\left[Q^{\otimes mk/2}\otimes(Q^{*})^{\otimes mk/2}\right]=M_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}^{(mk/2)},

(92)

which is the
mk/2
-th moment (super-)operator, i.e., the vectorized version of the
mk/2
-th twirl
Mele 2024
. This is possible as
Q
is real, i.e.,
Q\in\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
, and hence
Q^{*}=Q
.
∎

B.2
Asymptotic moments via Weingarten calculus

Now that we have connected the moments in Eq. (
77
) to the moment super-operator
M_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}^{(mk/2)}
, we can leverage standard Weingarten-calculus techniques to compute them. Let us review the necessary machinery.

First we recall that the moment super-operator
M_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}^{(mk/2)}
is a projector onto the
mk/2
-fold commutant of
\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
, that is, the vector space spanned by the set of matrices

\{M\;|\;MQ^{\otimes mk/2}=Q^{\otimes mk/2}M\quad\forall Q\in\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)\}\,.

(93)

In particular, the
mk/2
-fold commutant of
\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
consists of a representation of the Brauer algebra
\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}(2n)

Collins and Matsumoto 2017
when
\frac{mk}{2}<n
. When
\frac{mk}{2}\geq n
, an additional generator must be added to those of
\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}(2n)
to obtain the commutant
Grood 1999
. Note that this additional generator does not appear in the commutant of the orthogonal group
\mathbb{O}(2n)
.

The Brauer algebra
\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}(2n)
is a vector space spanned by a basis consisting of all possible pairings of a set of size
mk
, endowed with an additional bilinear operation (product). That is, given a set of
mk
items, the basis elements of the Brauer algebra correspond to all possible ways of splitting them into pairs.
This implies that all the permutations in the symmetric group
S_{mk/2}
are also in
\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}(2n)
, as these correspond to the pairings that can only connect the first
mk/2
items to the remainder ones. A straightforward calculation reveals that there are
\frac{(mk)!}{2^{mk/2}(mk/2)!}=(mk-1)!!
elements in the aforementioned basis of the Brauer algebra. Here we also note that every basis element
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}(2n)
can be completely specified by
mk/2
disjoint pairs, as

\sigma=\{\{\lambda_{1},\sigma(\lambda_{1})\}\cup\dots\cup\{\lambda_{mk/2},\sigma(\lambda_{mk/2})\}\}\,.

(94)

In Supp. Fig.
B.2
, we diagrammatically show all the elements
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}(2n)
for
\frac{mk}{2}=1,2
(as well as some for
\frac{mk}{2}=3
) using tensor representation. The product is bilinear and associative (so the Brauer algebra is an associative algebra), and can be defined graphically on the basis elements by concatenation, see Supp. Fig.
B.2
. . When closed loops appear, the resulting basis element gets multiplied by
2n
raised to the number of loops. Furthermore, the transpose of an element
\sigma
of the Brauer algebra is defined as
\sigma^{\top}=\{\{\lambda_{1}+mk/2,\sigma(\lambda_{1})+mk/2\}\cup\dots\cup\{\lambda_{mk/2}+mk/2,\sigma(\lambda_{mk/2})+mk/2\}\}
, where the sum is taken mod
mk/2

(graphically,
\sigma^{\top}
is the mirror image of
\sigma
).

Supplemental Figure B.2:
Tensor representation of the basis elements of the Brauer algebra.
Tensor diagram showing the unvectorized and vectorized forms of all the elements
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}(2n)
for
\frac{mk}{2}=1,2
, and some for
\frac{mk}{2}=3
.

While the previous paragraph determines how the abstract Brauer algebra is defined, we still need to specify how its elements are represented and how they act on
\mathcal{H}^{\otimes mk}
. In particular, we here consider the representation
F_{2n}:\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}(2n)\rightarrow\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}^{\otimes mk/2})
such that

F_{d}(\sigma)=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{mk}=1}^{2n}\ket{i_{mk/2+1},i_{t+2},\dots,i_{mk}}\bra{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{mk/2}}\,\prod_{\gamma=1}^{mk/2}\delta_{i_{\lambda_{\gamma}},i_{\sigma(\lambda_{\gamma})}}\,,

(95)

and its vectorization

\ket{F_{d}(\sigma)}\!\rangle=\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{mk}=1}^{2n}\ket{i_{mk/2+1},i_{t+2},\dots,i_{mk}}\ket{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{mk/2}}\,\prod_{\gamma=1}^{mk/2}\delta_{i_{\lambda_{\gamma}},i_{\sigma(\lambda_{\gamma})}}\,.

(96)

Note that the transpose
\sigma^{\top}
in the vectorization
\ket{F_{d}(\sigma^{\top})}\!\rangle
is graphically achieved by exchanging the top and bottom half indices/legs of the vectorization
\ket{F_{d}(\sigma)}\!\rangle
.

Given these definitions, the following important lemma holds.

Lemma B.3

(Asymptotic moment operator)
.

The (vectorized) moment operator as defined in Eq. (
88
) is given by

M_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}^{(mk/2)}=\frac{1}{(2n)^{mk/2}}\left(\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}}\ket{F_{2n}(\sigma^{\top})}\!\rangle\!\langle\!\bra{F_{2n}(\sigma)}+\!\!\!\sum_{\sigma,\pi\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}}\!\!\!w_{\sigma,\pi}\ket{F_{2n}(\sigma)}\!\rangle\!\langle\!\bra{F_{2n}(\pi)}\right)\,,

(97)

when
mk<2n
, with
w_{\sigma,\pi}\in\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)
.

Proof.

The moment operator for the orthogonal group can be found in e.g., Ref.
García-Martín et al. 2025
(see Supplementary Theorem 6), and the proof that it is equal to that of the special orthogonal group when
mk<2n
in Ref.
Grood 1999
.
Writing it in vectorized form readily yields Eq. (
97
).
∎

In conclusion, the moment operator in Lemma
B.3
expresses the
k
-th moments (for
mk
even) over
\mathbb{SO}(2n)
in terms of Brauer algebra representations. Combining this result with Lemma
B.2
, we arrive at

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]=\frac{1}{(2n)^{mk/2}}\Biggl(

\displaystyle\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}}\langle\!\braket{\bm{O}|{}^{\otimes k}|F_{2n}(\sigma^{\top})}\!\rangle\!\langle\!\braket{F_{2n}(\sigma)|\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}|\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle

\displaystyle+\!\sum_{\sigma,\pi\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}}w_{\sigma,\pi}\langle\!\braket{\bm{O}|{}^{\otimes k}|F_{2n}(\sigma)}\!\rangle\!\langle\!\braket{F_{2n}(\pi)|\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}|\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle\Biggr)\,,

(98)

as obtained from direct substitution of Eq. (
97
) in Eq. (
87
).
A tensor-network diagram of this moment operator expression is provided in Supp. Fig.
B.3
, analogous to the previous
\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
Haar-random diagram in Supp. Fig.
B.1
.
This diagrammatic approach will become useful to prove the main theorem in the subsequent section.

Supplemental Figure B.3:
Tensor-network diagram for asymptotic Weingarten-calculus moment expression.
Depiction in vectorized form with sums over basis elements of the Brauer algebra
\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}
representation,
F_{2n}(\sigma)
, as in Eq. (
B.2
). While the first sum applies Brauer elements
F_{2n}(\sigma^{T})
and
F_{2n}(\sigma)
to the observable and state tensors
\bm{O}
and
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}
, related via transposition, independent Brauer elements
\sigma,\pi
act on them in the second, asymptotically subordinate, sum. One specific pair of Brauer elements is represented for each sum.

B.3
From moments to Gaussian processes

Next, we determine conditions on the observable
O
and states
\rho_{i_{j}}
under which Haar-random matchgate transformations induce quantum Gaussian processes in the large-
n
limit.
This connection is established through Isserlis’s theorem
Isserlis 1918
, which provides the moments of a
gp
(multivariate Gaussian distribution) of any order
k
. Consider a zero-mean multivariate Gaussian random vector
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{N}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)
. If
k
is odd, the moment vanishes

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]=0\,,

(99)

and if
k
is even, the moments are uniquely determined by the covariances (second-order moments) of possible partitionings of the
k
random variables into pairs. This reads as

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]=\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{k/2}}\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\sigma}\operatorname{\mathrm{Cov}}\left[X_{i_{j}},X_{i_{j^{\prime}}}\right]\,,

(100)

where
\operatorname{\mathrm{Cov}}\left[X_{i},X_{i^{\prime}}\right]=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{i}X_{i^{\prime}}\right]
.

To establish the conditions and proofs for the emergence of GPs, we analyze the moments in terms of Brauer algebra contractions of the observable and state tensors,
\bm{O}
and
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}
, respectively. This analysis relies on the following contraction property:

Definition B.1

(Pairwise tensor matchings)
.

Consider a contraction over
k
rank-
m
tensors
\{\bm{A}^{(j)}\}_{j=1}^{k}
with index pairings indicated by
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}
, denoted as

\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\bm{A}^{(j)}\right).

(101)

We say that
\sigma
provides a pairwise tensor matching if all the indices of each tensor are contracted with the indices of exactly one other tensor. Formally,
\sigma
can be decomposed into a tensor-level pairing, described by
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{B}_{k/2}
, and an index-level permutation
\pi_{j,j^{\prime}}\in S_{m}
for each of the
k/2
tensor pairs
(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}
, such that

\sigma=\bm{\sigma}\circ\bigotimes_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}}\pi_{j,j^{\prime}}\,.

(102)

Otherwise,
\sigma
is referred to as a non-pairwise tensor matching
7
7

7

Also denoted as
\neg
pairwise,
\neg
pair, or
\neg\,\sigma\,\mathrm{pairwise}
.
.

Supplemental Fig.
B.4
illustrates tensor network diagrams for both pairwise (a) and non-pairwise (b) matchings, as well as the decomposition (c) into tensor-level pairings and index-level permutations as per Eq. (
102
).

We now state two formal conditions for the formation of matchgate quantum Gaussian processes:

Condition 1

(Full module support of observable)
.

The observable must have full support on the module
\mathcal{B}_{m}
(
m\neq 0,2n
), such that

O\in\mathcal{B}_{m}\quad\iff\quad\left\lVert O\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}=\left\lVert O\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathcal{B}}\,.

(103)

This first condition simplifies the analysis by constraining the observable to a single module. The next condition guarantees that in the asymptotic limit of large Hilbert-space dimension, the moments converge to those of a multivariate Gaussian distribution.

Condition 2

(Gaussian pairings dominate)
.

For any even
k\geq 4
and all multisets
\{\rho_{i_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{k}
, let

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{pair}}

\displaystyle=\quad\sum_{\mathclap{\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{B}_{k/2}}}

\displaystyle\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}^{k}\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}}\braket{\rho_{i_{j}}\,,\rho_{i_{j^{\prime}}}}_{\mathcal{B}_{m}},

(104)

\displaystyle S_{\neg\mathrm{pair}}

\displaystyle=\quad\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}\\
\neg\,\sigma\,\mathrm{pairwise}\end{subarray}}}\quad

\displaystyle\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma^{\top}}\!\left(\bm{O}^{\otimes k}\right)\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}\!\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{\left(i_{j}\right)}\right)\,,

(105)

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{mix}}

\displaystyle=\quad\sum_{\mathclap{\sigma,\pi\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}}}

\displaystyle w_{\sigma,\pi}\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}\!\left(\bm{O}^{\otimes k}\right)\operatorname{contr}_{\pi}\!\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{\left(i_{j}\right)}\right)\,,

(106)

with
w_{\sigma,\pi}\in\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)
as in Eq. (
B.2
).

In the limit
n\to\infty
, the sum
S_{\mathrm{pair}}
must then asymptotically (strictly) dominate
8
8

8

Recall that the asymptotic bound applies to the magnitude; i.e.,
f(n)\in o(g(n))\iff|f(n)|\in o(g(n))
.
the sums
S_{\neg\mathrm{pair}}
and
S_{\mathrm{mix}}
, i.e.,

\frac{S_{\neg\mathrm{pair}}}{S_{\mathrm{pair}}}\in o(1)\,,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{S_{\mathrm{mix}}}{S_{\mathrm{pair}}}\in o(1)\,.

(107)

This is the formal statement of the informal “Gaussian pairings dominate” condition in the main text.
Note that Supp. Info.
B.4
provides simpler alternative, both equivalent and sufficient, formulations to Condition
2
.

Supplemental Figure B.4:

Pairwise and non-pairwise tensor matchings and pairwise matching decomposition.
Lines indicate connected tensor legs at the index level, whereas shaded backgrounds represent tensor-level matchings. (a, b) Index pairing examples (
m=2
,
k=4
) yielding (a) a pairwise tensor matching between the first and last tensor pairs, and (b) a non-pairwise matching that connects all four tensors without pairwise factorization.
(c) Decomposition of a Brauer element
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}
representing a pairwise tensor matching (
m=4
,
k=6
), as defined in Eq. (
102
). The horizontal dashed red line and arrows indicate the action of the transposition, which swaps the top and bottom halves of the indices. Transposing
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}
inverts only those index-level permutations
\pi_{j,j^{\prime}}\in S_{m}
with corresponding tensor pairings
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{B}_{k/2}
that cross this line.

Under these conditions, we derive explicit expressions for the moments, distinguishing between even and odd moments.

Lemma B.4

(Even moments)
.

Under Conditions
1
and
2
, and in the large-
n
limit, the even
k
-th moments are

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]=\sum_{\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{B}_{k/2}}\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}}\underbrace{\frac{m!}{(2n)^{m}}\left\lVert O\right\rVert^{2}_{m}\left\langle\rho_{i_{j}},\rho_{i_{j^{\prime}}}\right\rangle_{m}}_{=\operatorname{\mathrm{Cov}}(X_{i_{j}},X_{i_{j^{\prime}}})}\,.

(108)

Proof.

Given Condition
1
, the observable
O
is fully supported on the module
\mathcal{B}_{m}
and, hence, allows us to apply Lemma
B.2
to the even
k
-th moments
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)}\left[X_{i_{1}}X_{i_{2}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]
. Specifically, since
k
is even,
mk
is even, such that Lemma
B.2
applies to express the moment through the moment operator,

\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]=\langle\!\bra{\bm{O}}^{\otimes k}\;M_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}^{(mk/2)}\;\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\ket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle.

(109)

Recall that
\ket{\bm{O}}\!\rangle^{\otimes k}
and
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\ket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle
are the vectorized module (
\mathcal{B}_{m}
) coefficient tensors for the observable
O
and the states
\rho_{i_{j}}
, respectively.
As we are interested in the large-
n
limit, we further replace the moment operator
M_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}^{(mk/2)}
by its asymptotic expansion given by Lemma
B.3
, as

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2n)^{mk/2}}\Biggl(\phantom{{}+{}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}\\
\sigma\,\mathrm{pairwise}\end{subarray}}\langle\!\braket{\bm{O}|{}^{\otimes k}|F_{2n}(\sigma^{\top})}\!\rangle\!\langle\!\braket{F_{2n}(\sigma)|\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}|\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle

(110)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}=\frac{1}{(2n)^{mk/2}}\Biggl(}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}\\
\neg\,\sigma\,\mathrm{pairwise}\end{subarray}}\langle\!\braket{\bm{O}|{}^{\otimes k}|F_{2n}(\sigma^{\top})}\!\rangle\!\langle\!\braket{F_{2n}(\sigma)|\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}|\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle

(111)

\displaystyle\phantom{{}=\frac{1}{(2n)^{mk/2}}\Biggl(}+\!\sum_{\sigma,\pi\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}}w_{\sigma,\pi}\langle\!\braket{\bm{O}|{}^{\otimes k}|F_{2n}(\sigma)}\!\rangle\!\langle\!\braket{F_{2n}(\pi)|\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}|\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle\Biggr)\,,

(112)

where we additionally split the single summation over the Brauer algebra
\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}
into a sum with only pairwise tensor matchings on both
|\bm{O}\rangle\!\rangle^{\otimes k}
and
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}|\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}\rangle\!\rangle
(Eq. (
110
)), and another sum for the remaining matchings (Eq. (
111
)), which are non-pairwise.

The main part of the proof focuses on showing that such split is possible and that the sum in Eq. (
110
) matches, up to a factor
m!
,
S_{\mathrm{pair}}
in Condition
2
, Eq. (
104
). Simultaneously, this sum is shown to pick up the correct pre-factor to precisely match the even
k
-th moment in Eq. (
108
), which is the objective of this proof. To then conclude the proof, we apply Condition
2
to asymptotically strictly bound the other two sums in Eqs. (
111
) and (
112
) by
S_{\mathrm{pair}}
. Note that the dimension of the Brauer algebra, which determines the number of terms involved in these sums, does not depend on
n
and, hence, cannot impact the asymptotic order.

We note that for any Brauer algebra element
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}
that induces a pairwise tensor matching in
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\ket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}}\!\rangle
its transpose
\sigma^{\top}
simultaneously induces a pairwise tensor matching in
\ket{\bm{O}}\!\rangle^{\otimes k}
. Otherwise,
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\top}
simultaneously lead to non-pairwise tensor matchings. To show this property, consider the decomposition of a pairwise tensor matching
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}
into a tensor-level pairing
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{B}_{k/2}
and index-level permutations
\pi_{j,j^{\prime}}\in S_{m}
for each tensor pair
(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}
in Eq. (
102
).
The transposition
\sigma^{\top}
affects the decomposition as follows

\sigma^{\top}=\bm{\sigma}^{\top}\circ\bigotimes_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}^{\top}}\bar{\pi}_{j,j^{\prime}}\,,

(113)

with

\displaystyle\bm{\sigma}^{\top}

\displaystyle=\left\{(j+k/2,j^{\prime}+k/2)\!\!\!\!\mod{k}\;\middle|\;(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}\right\}\,,

(114)

\displaystyle\bar{\pi}_{j,j^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\pi_{j,j^{\prime}}\quad\text{if}\quad j,j^{\prime}\leq k/2\;\lor j,j^{\prime}>k/2\;\\
\pi_{j,j^{\prime}}^{-1}\quad\text{else}\end{cases}\,,

(115)

maintaining the structure of a pairwise matching of tensors because a decomposition is still possible. Supplemental Fig.
B.4
further exemplifies the effect of the transposition within the decomposition of the pairwise tensor matching.

Furthermore, multiple Brauer elements
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}
can resemble the same pairwise tensor matching
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{B}_{k/2}
, differing only in their index-level permutations
\pi_{j,j^{\prime}}\in S_{m}
. Remarkably, the contribution of any such
\sigma
in Eq. (
110
) is invariant under the choice of
\pi_{j,j^{\prime}}
.
To see why, recall that the contracted tensors are anti-symmetric, meaning an index permutation
\pi_{j,j^{\prime}}
introduces a (parity) sign,
\mathrm{sign}(\pi_{j,j^{\prime}})
. However, the overall contribution depends on both
\sigma
and its transpose
\sigma^{\top}
. As established in Eq. (
115
), the transpose acts on the index level by applying
\bar{\pi}_{j,j^{\prime}}
, which is either
\pi_{j,j^{\prime}}
or its inverse
\pi_{j,j^{\prime}}^{-1}
. Because the sign of a permutation is invariant under inversion (
\mathrm{sign}(\pi)=\mathrm{sign}(\pi^{-1})
), the combined sign from both contractions always trivially cancels as
\mathrm{sign}(\pi_{j,j^{\prime}})\mathrm{sign}(\bar{\pi}_{j,j^{\prime}})=1
.

Consequently, the permutation signs vanish entirely, and every choice of index-level permutations collapses identically to the non-permuted (id) contraction:

\displaystyle\operatorname{contr}_{\pi_{j,j^{\prime}}}(\bm{O},\bm{O})\operatorname{contr}_{\bar{\pi}_{j,j^{\prime}}}(\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{({i_{j}})},\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{({i_{j^{\prime}}})})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{contr}_{\mathrm{id}}(\bm{O},\bm{O})\operatorname{contr}_{\mathrm{id}}(\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{({i_{j}})},\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{({i_{j^{\prime}}})})

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{\bm{\nu}}\lvert o_{\bm{\nu}}\rvert^{2}\right)\left(\sum_{\bm{\nu}}(\rho_{\bm{\nu}}^{(i_{j})})^{*}\rho_{\bm{\nu}}^{(i_{j^{\prime}})}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\lVert O\right\rVert^{2}_{m}\langle\rho_{i_{j}},\rho_{i_{j^{\prime}}}\rangle_{m}\,.

(116)

This scalar contribution occurs independently for each of the
k/2
pairs
(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}
. Since the symmetric group
S_{m}
governs the index pairings, there are exactly
m!
valid choices for each
\pi_{j,j^{\prime}}
. Summing over all such permutation choices for a fixed tensor pairing
\bm{\sigma}
naturally scales the contribution by
(m!)^{k/2}
,

(m!)^{k/2}\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}}\left\lVert O\right\rVert^{2}_{m}\langle\rho_{i_{j}},\rho_{i_{j^{\prime}}}\rangle_{m}\,.

(117)

Including the global pre-factor
1/(2n)^{mk/2}
from Eq. (
110
), the total sum over all pairwise tensor matching contributions perfectly matches
S_{\mathrm{pair}}
(up to the scaling factor) and precisely recovers the even
k
-th moment given in Eq. (
108
) as

\frac{1}{(2n)^{mk/2}}\sum_{\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{B}_{k/2}}(m!)^{k/2}\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}}\left\lVert O\right\rVert^{2}_{m}\langle\rho_{i_{j}},\rho_{i_{j^{\prime}}}\rangle_{m}=\sum_{\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{B}_{k/2}}\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}}\frac{m!}{(2n)^{m}}\left\lVert O\right\rVert^{2}_{m}\langle\rho_{i_{j}},\rho_{i_{j^{\prime}}}\rangle_{m}\,.

(118)

To conclude the proof, observe that by the definition of the Brauer algebra representation
F_{2n}
in Eq. (
95
) and its vectorization
|F_{2n}\rangle\!\rangle
in Eq. (
96
), the vectorized inner product evaluates to
\langle\!\langle\bm{A}|F_{2n}(\sigma)\rangle\!\rangle=\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}(\bm{A})
for any rank-
mk

2n
-dimensional tensor (network)
\bm{A}
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}
. This equivalence is also visually apparent in the tensor network diagram of Suppl. Fig.
B.3
.
Therefore, the remaining sums, in Eqs. (
111
) and (
112
), constituting the
k
-th even moment map directly to
S_{\neg\mathrm{pair}}
and
S_{\mathrm{mix}}
, respectively. By Condition
2
, these terms are asymptotically strictly dominated by
S_{\mathrm{pair}}
. Thus, in the large-
n
limit, the contributions from
S_{\neg\mathrm{pair}}
and
S_{\mathrm{mix}}
vanish faster, leaving only the
S_{\mathrm{pair}}
term of leading order and allowing us to recover the general asymptotic form of the even
k
-th moments in Eq. (
108
).

∎

An important corollary can be obtained from Lemma
B.4
, regarding the covariance matrix of the variables
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
.

Corollary B.1

(Covariances)
.

The covariance of
X_{i}
and
X_{i^{\prime}}
is

\Sigma_{i,i^{\prime}}=\operatorname{\mathrm{Cov}}\left(X_{i},X_{i^{\prime}}\right)=\frac{m!}{(2n)^{m}}\lVert O\rVert^{2}\left\langle\rho_{i},\rho_{{i^{\prime}}}\right\rangle_{m}\,.

(119)

Proof.

Direct implication of Lemma
B.4
with
k=2
, leading to the trivial Brauer algebra
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
.
∎

Next, we present a proposition that automatically guarantees zero odd moments for some values of
m
and
k
.

Proposition B.1

(Guarantees for vanishing odd moments)
.

Under Condition
1
, odd
k
moments vanish (
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{U\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)}\left[X_{i_{1}}\cdots X_{i_{k}}\right]=0
) in any of the following cases:

(a)

When
k=1
(first moment).

(b)

When
m
is odd.

(c)

When
k=3
(third moment) and
m\equiv 2\pmod{4}
(i.e.,
m
is even but not a multiple of four).

Proof.

(a) The first moment (
k=1
) trivially vanishes as directly implied by Lemma
B.2
for odd
m
, and for even
m
, because all contractions in Eq. (
B.2
) are over individual anti-symmetric tensors, which are zero by definition.

(b) Vanishing odd moments generally in the odd
m
case are directly implied by Lemma
B.2
.

(c) Focusing on the third moment (
k=3
), any contraction in Eq. (
B.2
) defined by
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{{3m}/{2}}
either introduces self-loops (which vanish due to anti-symmetry) or splits the indices of each tensor into two groups of
m/2
indices, according to which other tensor they are connected to. By then reshaping the tensors into
\tfrac{m}{2}\times\tfrac{m}{2}
square matrices, we can express the contractions as matrix traces (up to a sign). Crucially, we only need to examine the contraction over the observable tensors, which corresponds to the trace
\lvert{\rm Tr}\left[O^{3}\right]\rvert
. Matrix transposition here corresponds to
m/2
swaps of tensor indices. Because
m\equiv 2\pmod{4}
, the number of swaps
m/2
is odd, meaning the reshaped matrix
O
is anti-symmetric. An anti-symmetric matrix raised to an odd power remains anti-symmetric and is therefore traceless, i.e.,
{\rm Tr}\left[O^{3}\right]=0
. Since every term in Eq. (
B.2
) contains an observable contraction factor, the entire third moment exactly vanishes.
∎

Note that all odd moments are also algebraically guaranteed to vanish for a special class of observables within any module
\mathcal{B}_{m}
, as those constructed as linear combinations of
\mathcal{B}_{m}
basis operators associated with mutually disjoint Majorana indices (see Proposition
C.5
in Supp. Info.
C.4
).

Proposition
B.1
outlines where odd moments inherently vanish due to the algebraic structure of their contractions, but these guarantees are incomplete for even (fermionic) modules
\mathcal{B}_{m}
. For the third moment (
k=3
), reshaping the tensors into matrices allows the contractions to be evaluated as traces. While this reshaping yields anti-symmetric matrices with naturally vanishing traces when
m\equiv 2\pmod{4}
, modules with
m\equiv 0\pmod{4}
involve an even number of index swaps (
m/2
). This results in symmetric matrices whose traces do not inherently vanish, meaning that a zero third moment relies entirely on specific system properties (e.g.,
{\rm Tr}\left[O^{3}\right]=0
). Furthermore, this matrix reshaping technique is strictly limited to
k=3
, while for higher odd moments (
k\geq 5
), a single tensor can contract with more than two others, breaking the cyclic structure required to express the contraction as a matrix trace and preventing straightforward algebraic arguments, thus necessitating a case-by-case analysis.
Comparing the explicit expressions for the moments in Lemma
B.4
to the moments of a Gaussian process (via Isserlis theorem
Isserlis 1918
), we derive the following two main theorems that certify whether Gaussian processes are formed or not, respectively (with some cases covered by Prop.
B.1
):

Theorem 1

(Gaussian Process)
.

Under Conditions
1
,
2
and in the large-
n
limit,
\left\{X_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{N}
forms a
gp
if and only if all odd moments vanish.

Proof.

Clearly, since Lemma
B.4
holds under Conditions
1
and
2
and in the large-
n
limit, the form of the even moments as specified in Eq. (
108
) then matches the form in Eq. (
100
) of Isserlis’s theorem
Isserlis 1918
. Furthermore, the zero odd moments align with Isserlis’s theorem.
Finally, to prove that these matching moments uniquely determine the joint distribution of the
X_{i}
random variables, we can use Carleman’s condition.

Lemma B.5

(Carleman’s condition, Hamburger case
Kleiber and Stoyanov 2013
)
.

Let
\gamma_{k}
be the (finite) moments of the distribution of a random variable
X
that can take values on the real line
\mathbb{R}
. These moments determine uniquely the distribution of
X
if

\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\gamma_{2k}^{-1/2k}=\infty\,.

(120)

In our case, let

X=\operatorname{Tr}\left[U\rho U^{\dagger}O\right]\,,

(121)

and assume that
O\in B_{m}
(Condition
1
). Under the Gaussian-pairing-dominance condition (Condition
2
), the limiting moments of
X
are

\gamma_{2k-1}=0\,,\qquad\gamma_{2k}=(2k-1)!!\,\sigma^{2k}\,,

(122)

with

\sigma^{2}=\frac{m!}{(2n)^{m}}\,\|O\|_{m}^{2}\,\|\rho\|_{m}^{2}\,.

(123)

Then, for any
n
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\gamma_{2k}^{-1/(2k)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sigma}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\left((2k-1)!!\right)^{-1/(2k)}

(124)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\sigma}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(2k)^{-1/2}=\infty\,,

where we used
(2k-1)!!\leq(2k)^{k}
. Hence Carleman’s condition is satisfied, and the limiting univariate distribution is uniquely determined by its moments. Therefore,
X
converges in distribution to
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
. To conclude, we use the fact that since its marginal distributions are determinate via Carleman’s condition, then so is the joint distribution of
\{X_{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}

Kleiber and Stoyanov 2013
.

∎

Theorem 2

(No Gaussian Process)
.

Under Condition
1
and in the large-
n
limit,
\left\{X_{i}\right\}_{i=1}^{N}
does not form a
gp
if a) Condition
2
is violated, or b)
non-zero odd moments occur.

Proof.

By the contrapositive of Isserlis’s theorem
Isserlis 1918
, a distribution is not a zero-mean Gaussian if its even moments deviate from the pairwise sum in Eq. (
100
), or if its odd moments are non-zero. The theorem follows immediately:

a)

Violating Condition
2
implies that non-pairwise tensor matchings contribute at the leading asymptotic order. Consequently, the even moments diverge from the pairwise form of Eq. (
108
), violating Isserlis’s theorem.

b)

The presence of non-zero odd moments trivially violates the definition of a zero-mean Gaussian process.

∎

B.4
General implications and simplifications

We now provide simplified, equivalent formulations of Condition
2
for the univariate case, and establish an alternative sufficient condition. Furthermore, we derive broad implications by restricting our focus to the
\mathcal{B}_{1}
module and introduce verifiable equivalent conditions for the
\mathcal{B}_{2}
module. Note that further simplifications may be achieved by combining insights from the alternative formulations presented here.

B.4.1
Equivalent univariate condition

Proposition B.2

(Equivalent univariate formulation of Condition
2
)
.

In the univariate case, i.e., single state
\rho
being considered, Condition
2
is equivalent to the following condition:

For every even
k\geq 4
,

\forall\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2},\;\neg\,\sigma\,\mathrm{pairwise}\colon\,\frac{\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma^{\top}}\!\left(\bm{O}^{\otimes k}\right)}{\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}^{k}}\frac{\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}\!\left(\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{\otimes k}\right)}{\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}^{k}}\in o(1)\,,

(125)

and

\forall\sigma,\pi\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}\colon\,\frac{\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}\!\left(\bm{O}^{\otimes k}\right)}{\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}^{k}}\frac{\operatorname{contr}_{\pi}\!\left(\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{\otimes k}\right)}{\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}^{k}}\in o\left(\frac{1}{w_{\sigma,\pi}}\right)\,,

(126)

where
1/w_{\sigma,\pi}\in\Omega(n)
as defined in Eq. (
97
).

Proof.

Analogous to the observable, in the univariate case, the module overlaps for the states reduce to module purities as

S_{\mathrm{pair}}=\dim(\mathfrak{B}_{k/2})\left(\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{m}^{2}\left\lVert\rho\right\rVert_{{}_{m}}^{2}\right)^{k/2}\,.

(127)

It is important to note that
S_{\mathrm{pair}}\geq 0
so that, instead of bounding the ratios of the full sums
S_{\neg\mathrm{pair}}
and
S_{\mathrm{mix}}
, we can equivalently bound all the summands (recall that the sums iterate over the Brauer algebra basis, with dimension always independent of the number of qubits
n
). Therefore, we obtain that
{S_{\neg\mathrm{pair}}}/{S_{\mathrm{pair}}}\in o(1)
is equivalent to Eq. (
125
) and
{S_{\mathrm{mix}}}/{S_{\mathrm{pair}}}\in o(1)
is equivalent to Eq. (
126
), where the latter condition was additionally rearranged by dividing by
w_{\sigma,\pi}
factors.
∎

B.4.2
Sufficient conditions

While the non-negativity of the module purity/norm allowed for an equivalent simplified formulation of Condition
2
in the univariate case, for the multivariate case, such simplification can only be achieved by requiring non-negative module overlaps, resulting in the following strictly stronger sufficient condition:

Proposition B.3

(Sufficient non-negative module overlap formulation of Condition
2
)
.

Condition
2
is fulfilled if the following sufficient condition holds:

All states exhibit non-negative module overlaps

\forall\rho_{i},\rho_{i^{\prime}}\colon\braket{\rho_{i},\rho_{i^{\prime}}}_{m}\geq 0\,,

(128)

and, for every even
k\geq 4
,

\forall\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2},\;\neg\,\sigma\,\mathrm{pairwise}\;\exists\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{B}_{k/2}\colon\,\frac{\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma^{\top}}\!\bigl(\bm{O}^{\otimes k}\bigr)}{\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{m}^{k}}\frac{\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}\!\bigl(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{\left(i_{j}\right)}\bigr)}{\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}}\langle{\rho_{i_{j}},\rho_{i_{j^{\prime}}}}\rangle_{m}}\in o(1)\,,

(129)

and

\forall\sigma,\pi\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}\;\exists\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{B}_{k/2}\colon\,\frac{\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}\!\bigl(\bm{O}^{\otimes k}\bigr)}{\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{m}^{k}}\frac{\operatorname{contr}_{\pi}\!\bigl(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{\left(i_{j}\right)}\bigr)}{\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}}\langle{\rho_{i_{j}},\rho_{i_{j^{\prime}}}}\rangle_{m}}\in o\left(\frac{1}{w_{\sigma,\pi}}\right)\,,

(130)

where
1/w_{\sigma,\pi}\in\Omega(n)
as defined in Eq. (
97
).

Proof.

Analogous to the proof of Proposition
B.2
, the required non-negativity of the module overlaps (
\braket{\rho_{i},\rho_{i^{\prime}}}_{m}\geq 0
) ensures that every summand in
S_{\mathrm{pair}}
(Eq. (
104
)) is strictly non-negative. Consequently, the full sum
S_{\mathrm{pair}}
is lower-bounded by any single one of its summands, corresponding to a specific pairwise matching
\bm{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{B}_{k/2}
.

Therefore, to show that
S_{\neg\mathrm{pair}}/S_{\mathrm{pair}}\in o(1)
and
S_{\mathrm{mix}}/S_{\mathrm{pair}}\in o(1)
, it suffices to show that
every
individual term in
S_{\neg\mathrm{pair}}
and
S_{\mathrm{mix}}
is asymptotically dominated by
at least one
corresponding term from
S_{\mathrm{pair}}
. Because the number of elements in the Brauer algebra (and thus the number of summands) is independent of
n
, this term-by-term bounding guarantees the asymptotic dominance of the entire sum. Equations (
129
) and (
130
) precisely formalize this term-by-term domination by asserting the existence of such a tensor bounding
\bm{\sigma}
for every
\sigma
and
\pi
.
∎

Alternatively, by relaxing the explicit weights
w_{\sigma,\pi}
to their asymptotic upper bound of
\mathcal{O}(1)
, we can merge
S_{\neg\mathrm{pair}}
and
S_{\mathrm{mix}}
into a single, strictly stronger sufficient condition.

Proposition B.4

(Sufficient unified single-sum formulation of Condition
2
)
.

Condition
2
is satisfied if
S_{\mathrm{pair}}
asymptotically dominates the unweighted sum of all contractions involving at least one non-pairwise matching:

\frac{\sum_{{\begin{subarray}{c}\sigma,\pi\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}\\
\neg(\sigma\,\mathrm{pairwise}\,\wedge\,\pi\,\mathrm{pairwise})\end{subarray}}}\operatorname{contr}_{\pi}\!\bigl(\bm{O}^{\otimes k}\bigr)\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}\!\bigl(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{\left(i_{j}\right)}\bigr)}{S_{\mathrm{pair}}}\in o(1)\,.

(131)

Proof.

S_{\neg\mathrm{pair}}
consists of non-pairwise terms (
\pi^{\top}=\sigma
, weight 1), while
S_{\mathrm{mix}}
contains cross-terms with weight
w_{\sigma,\pi}\in\mathcal{O}(1/n)
. Relaxing these weights to
\mathcal{O}(1)
and bounding their combined sum strictly upper-bounds both
S_{\neg\mathrm{pair}}
and
S_{\mathrm{mix}}
. We can safely exclude cross-terms where both
\sigma
and
\pi
are pairwise since their raw contractions scale as
\mathcal{O}(S_{\mathrm{pair}})
, so their
\mathcal{O}(1/n)
weights inherently guarantee they vanish as
o(1)
relative to
S_{\mathrm{pair}}
.
∎

B.4.3
Module
\mathcal{B}_{1}
general implications

For the smallest non-trivial module
\mathcal{B}_{1}
(i.e.,
m=1
), Condition
2
always holds and we generally obtain GPs, both in the univariate (
N=1
) and multivariate (
N>1
) cases:

Corollary B.2
.

For observables
O\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
,
under Condition
1
and in the large-
n
limit,
\left\{X_{i_{j}}\right\}_{j=1}^{N}
forms a
gp
.

Proof.

First, we note that Condition
2
(which must be fulfilled for Lemma
B.4
to apply) is trivially fulfilled since any contraction from
\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}
for
m=1
over
k
tensors results only in pairwise tensor matchings as per the definition of the Brauer algebra
\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}
. This is valid for both distinct tensors and copies of the same tensor, i.e., multivariate and univariate scenarios, respectively.
Specifically, when viewing these rank-1 tensors
\bm{O}
and
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}
as vectors, it is clear that any contraction leads to scalar products of pairs of such tensors.
Second, the odd number of Majoranas (
m=1
) makes the odd moments trivially vanish as per Prop.
B.1
. Therefore, Theorem
1
generally applies and predicts
gp
.
∎

Indeed, we show that the asymptotic kernel function (covariance) matches the exact non-asymptotic expression for
\mathcal{B}_{1}
. Instead of using the asymptotic moment operator in Eq. (
97
), we use the exact Weingarten coefficient of
1/(2n)
for the corresponding moment operator over the orthogonal group
M_{\mathbb{O}(2n)}^{(1)}=M_{\mathbb{SO}(2n)}^{(1)}
, as provided in Eq. (D7) in the Supplemental Information of Ref.
García-Martín et al. 2025
. The result reads

\Sigma_{t,t^{\prime}}=\kappa\left(\rho_{t},\rho_{t^{\prime}}\right)=\frac{1}{2n}\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}^{2}\left\langle\rho_{t},\rho_{{t^{\prime}}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{B}_{1}}\,,

(132)

and matches the covariance in Eq. (
308
), which was derived from the asymptotic tools.

B.4.4
Module
\mathcal{B}_{2}
general implications

For
m=2
, the rank-
m
coefficient tensors of the projection onto the module
\mathcal{B}_{2}
of the observables and states,
\bm{O}
and
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}
can be reshaped as
2n\times 2n
real square matrices, denoted as
O
and
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j})}
. The anti-symmetry is directly inherited, as
A=-A^{\top}
. Here, we leverage the fact that the ratios in the univariate equivalent formulation of Condition
2
in Proposition
B.2
simplify to Schatten 1- vs 2-norm ratios (denoted as
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{2}
and
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{1}
, respectively) in this case:

Proposition B.5

(Equivalent univariate formulation of Condition
2
for
\mathcal{B}_{2}
)
.

In the univariate
\mathcal{B}_{2}
case, Condition
2
is equivalent to

\frac{\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{1}}\cdot\frac{\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\right\rVert_{1}}\in o(1)\,,

(133)

with
\mathbb{O}=O^{\top}O
and
{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}=\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{\top}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}
.
Since each fraction alone is at most one,
strict asymptotic vanishing, i.e.,
o(1)
, for one fraction suffices for Eq. (
133
) to hold.

Proof.

A contraction over rank-2 tensors forms cycles of tensors, where such a cycle can always be expressed as the trace of a product of matrices. Disjoint contractions may occur, i.e., disjoint cycles, which relate to products of such traces of matrix products.

First, consider
k=4
for Condition
2
in the formulation of Proposition
B.2
, the ratio of Eq. (
125
) simplifies to

\left\lvert\frac{{\rm Tr}\left[O^{\top}OO^{\top}O\right]}{{\rm Tr}\left[O^{\top}O\right]^{2}}\cdot\frac{{\rm Tr}\left[\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{\top}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{\top}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}\right]}{{\rm Tr}\left[\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{\top}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}\right]^{2}}\right\rvert

(134)

for any non-pairwise
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}
.
Note that, without loss of generality, the transposition can be applied and fixed arbitrarily since the matrices are invariant up to a sign due to the anti-symmetry, and the sign is irrelevant for the absolute value of the products and fractions. Moreover, since any non-zero disjoint contraction over
k=4
tensors directly corresponds to two pairwise tensor matchings, such contractions are excluded in the numerator.
Let

\mathbb{O}=O^{\top}O

and
{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}=\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{\top}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}

, which are positive semidefinite.
Due to this positive semidefiniteness,
\lvert\mathbb{A}\rvert=\mathbb{A}
holds, where
|\cdot\rvert
is defined with respect to the operator square root as
|\mathbb{A}\rvert=\sqrt{\mathbb{A}^{\dagger}\mathbb{A}}
.
Therefore, the traces in Eq. (
134
) match the definition of (
p
-th powers of) the Schatten
p
-norm

\left\lVert\mathbb{A}\right\rVert_{p}^{p}={\rm Tr}\left[\left\lvert\mathbb{A}\right\rvert^{p}\right]\,,

(135)

for
p=1
(trace norm) and
p=2

hs
norm. In terms of Schatten norms Eq. (
134
) reads then as

\frac{\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}}\cdot\frac{\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}}\,,

(136)

and, therefore, directly yields Eq. (
133
) as an equivalent condition for the case
k=4
in Eq. (
125
) in Proposition
B.2
.

It remains to show that any higher (even)
k>4
for Condition
2
and equivalently its univariate formulation in Proposition
B.2
automatically holds if this condition (Eq. (
133
)) is satisfied, i.e., for
k=4
.
The (Schatten) norm monotonicity property is relevant for the following derivation, which is formally
decreasing monotonicity in
p
, i.e.,
\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{1}\geq\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{p}\geq\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{p^{\prime}}\geq\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\infty}
in
1\leq p\leq p^{\prime}\leq\infty
.
Now, the ratios of Eq. (
125
) in Proposition
B.2
for any even
k>4
can again be expressed in terms of Schatten norm ratios and related to the simplified
k=4
condition in Eq. (
133
). Considering contractions in the numerators that match all
k
tensors, we recover the
k=4
condition as

\left\lvert\frac{{\rm Tr}\left[\mathbb{O}^{k/2}\right]}{{\rm Tr}\left[\mathbb{O}\right]^{k/2}}\frac{{\rm Tr}\left[{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}^{k/2}\right]}{{\rm Tr}\left[{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\right]^{k/2}}\right\rvert=\frac{\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{k/2}^{k/2}}{\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{1}^{k/2}}\frac{\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\right\rVert_{k/2}^{k/2}}{\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\right\rVert_{1}^{k/2}}=\left(\frac{\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{k/2}}{\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{1}}\frac{\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\right\rVert_{k/2}}{\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\right\rVert_{1}}\right)^{k/2}\leq\left(\frac{\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{1}}\frac{\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\right\rVert_{1}}\right)^{k/2}\in o(1)\,.

(137)

Otherwise, if the contractions are still non-pairwise tensor matchings but disjoint, the ratios of
k
tensor contractions split into the product of ratios of
k_{l}<k
. Hence, such contractions yield products of traces of
k_{l}
powers of (anti-symmetric) matrices. If any
k_{l}
is odd, the entire ratio must vanish as an odd power of an anti-symmetric matrix is anti-symmetric and, hence, traceless.
For only even
k_{l}
, apply Eq. (
137
) for each
k_{l}
instead of
k
individually. Overall, any even
k>4
ratio of Condition
2
is always upper bounded by the Schatten 2 vs 1-norm ratio in Eq. (
133
).

The final note that each fraction is bounded by
1
follows directly from the same monotonicity (
\left\lVert\mathbb{A}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\left\lVert\mathbb{A}\right\rVert_{1}
). Finally, this
\mathcal{O}(1)
upper bound on all contraction ratios automatically satisfies the mixed term condition in Eq. (
126
), as the constant left-hand side is trivially strictly dominated by the inverse weights
1/w_{\sigma,\pi}\in\Omega(n)
.

∎

In addition, for the special case of
m=2
, the general odd moment statement of Proposition
B.1
can be extended as follows.

Corollary B.3

(Vanishing odd moments)
.

In the
\mathcal{B}_{2}
case and under Condition
1
, all odd moments vanish.

Proof.

The moment operator involves linear combinations of contractions of an odd number
k
of module frame coefficient tensors
\{\bm{O}\}^{k}
and
\{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}^{(i_{j})}\}_{j=1}^{k}
, respectively. As opposed to general
m
, for
m=2
as in the previous Corollary, any contraction of the anti-symmetric rank-
2
tensors can be decomposed into products of traces of anti-symmetric matrices through re-shaping of the tensors. This product contains at least one trace with an odd number of such matrices. Therefore, this trace vanishes since the product of an odd number of identical (hence commuting) anti-symmetric matrices is an anti-symmetric matrix with zero trace generally. This is the case for both the univariate and multivariate cases, as the matrix associated with the observable occurs identically in the product multiple times. Hence, any such contraction vanishes, which concludes the proof.
∎

Concluding the case of module
\mathcal{B}_{2}
, we provide the exact covariance expression, which resembles the (exact) kernel function to build the quantum Gaussian process model. Instead of using the asymptotic moment operator in Eq. (
97
) of Lemma
B.3
to calculate the (asymptotic) second moment, we use the exact Weingarten coefficients for the corresponding moment operator over the orthogonal group
M_{\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)}^{(2)}
in Eq. (D10) in the supplemental material of Ref.
García-Martín et al. 2025
, yielding

\Sigma_{t,t^{\prime}}=\kappa\left(\rho_{t},\rho_{t^{\prime}}\right)=\frac{1}{n(2n-1)}\left\lVert O\right\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}^{2}\left\langle\rho_{t},\rho_{{t^{\prime}}}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}\,.

(138)

Note that, as opposed to the case of
\mathcal{B}_{1}
, we now observe a deviation from the asymptotic expression, cf. Eq. (
308
). However, as expected for the second moments and the absence of non-pairwise tensor contractions as discussed in the proof of Lemma
B.4
, this deviation manifests itself purely in a scaling factor.
As expected, the exact factor is simply the inverse of the module dimension
\dim(\mathcal{B}_{2})=\binom{2n}{2}=n(2n-1)
, while the asymptotic factor relies on the inverse of the upper bound
\dim(\mathcal{B}_{2})\leq 2n^{2}
. Overall, the relative deviation is
1-1/(2n)
.

B.4.5
Module isomorphism

We can exploit the module isomorphism
\mathcal{B}_{2n-m}\simeq\mathcal{B}_{m}
to directly transfer all
gp
statements between the modules
\mathcal{B}_{2n-m}
and
\mathcal{B}_{m}
, i.e., between products of
2n-m
and
m
Majorana operators. In particular, the isomorphic map between modules, denoted as
\Gamma(\cdot)=\cdot\,\Gamma
, is achieved through multiplication by
\Gamma=i^{n}C_{1}C_{2}\cdots C_{2n}
. It is easy to see that
C_{i_{1}}C_{i_{2}}\cdots C_{i_{m}}\Gamma
gives a product of Majoranas in
\mathcal{B}_{2n-m}
(up to a phase), and that multiplication by
\Gamma
is its own inverse, i.e.,
\Gamma^{2}=\openone
. This readily identifies
\mathcal{B}_{2n-m}
and
\mathcal{B}_{m}
as vector spaces. But more importantly,
\Gamma(\cdot)
identifies
\mathcal{B}_{2n-m}
and
\mathcal{B}_{m}
as
\mathbb{SPIN}(2n)
isomorphic modules: indeed, since
[U,\Gamma]=0
for all matchgate unitaries
U
, it follows that
\Gamma\left(UC_{\mu}U^{\dagger}\right)=U\Gamma\left(C_{\mu}\right)U^{\dagger}
, so
\Gamma(\cdot)
is equivariant and hence compatible with the
\mathbb{SPIN}(2n)
group action.

For the analysis of quantum Gaussian processes, we need to establish how the moments transform under
\Gamma
. Considering the transformed observable and state components
O\Gamma
and
\rho_{i_{j}}\Gamma
, then the moments are identical,

\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathbb{SPIN}(2n)}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{k}{\rm Tr}\left[\Gamma OU\rho_{i_{j}}\Gamma U^{\dagger}\right]\right]=\mathbb{E}_{U\sim\mathbb{SPIN}(2n)}\left[\prod_{j=1}^{k}{\rm Tr}\left[OU\rho_{i_{j}}U^{\dagger}\right]\right]\,,

(139)

where we simply used that
\Gamma
is self-adjoint (
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\dagger})
and commutes with
U^{\dagger}
. Consequently, all statements regarding the convergence to Gaussian processes in the module
\mathcal{B}_{m}
can be automatically translated to the module
\mathcal{B}_{2n-m}
.

Supp. Info. C
Examples and special families of matchgate Gaussian processes

In this section, we verify common scenarios under which the conditions for the matchgate
qgp
(
qgp
) apply. This is specifically Condition
2
. Moreover, since violations of these conditions rule out
gp
behavior through Theorem
2
, we identify scenarios on a case-by-case basis for which an asymptotic deviation from
gp
is provable.

C.1
Special states and observables preliminaries

Because certain families of states and observables play a central role in the subsequent analyses, we first outline their key properties here.

C.1.1
Gaussian states

Pure (fermionic) Gaussian states are quantum states that can be prepared by a matchgate (free-fermionic) transformation
U
of a computational basis state
\ket{i},i\in[2^{n}]
, i.e.,

\ket{\psi}=U\ket{i}.

(140)

More narrowly defined, all Gaussian states of even (odd) parity can be prepared by applying a free-fermionic transformation to the all-zero basis state
\ket{0}^{\otimes n}
(respectively, to the first single-excitation state
\ket{10\ldots 0}=\ket{1}\ket{0}^{\otimes n-1}=X_{1}\ket{0}^{\otimes n}=C_{1}\ket{0}^{\otimes n}
). That is

\ket{\psi_{\rm even}}=U\ket{0}^{\otimes n}\quad\text{and}\quad\ket{\psi_{\rm odd}}=U\ket{10\ldots 0}.

(141)

Gaussian states of even and odd parity are also referred to as Gaussian states in the even and odd parity sectors, respectively. The even parity sector is spanned by (the union of) all even modules, while the odd parity sector is spanned by (the union of) all odd modules
Diaz et al. 2023
. Gaussian states are of high interest due to their connection to classical efficient simulability, which is that matchgate (free-fermionic) transformations of Gaussian states can be classically simulated in polynomial time
Jozsa and Miyake 2008
. Hence, as a necessary condition for classical intractability, states must be non-Gaussian.

We provide some further properties for even parity Gaussian states through their connection to the all-zero state
\rho_{0}=\left({\ket{0}\!\bra{0}}\right)^{\otimes n}
. First,
\rho_{0}
decomposes over products of the Pauli-Z strings as

\rho_{0}=\left(\frac{\openone+Z}{2}\right)^{\otimes n}=\frac{1}{d}\left(\openone+\sum_{i}Z_{i}+\sum_{i<j}Z_{i}Z_{j}+\sum_{i<j<k}Z_{i}Z_{j}Z_{k}+\ldots\right)\,,

(142)

with
d=2^{n}
.
One can now show that the frame coefficient tensor for any even module
\mathcal{B}_{m}
(with
m
even) of
\rho_{0}
can be expressed via direct sums of generalized (anti-symmetric) Kronecker delta tensors as
