\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m!d}}\bigoplus^{n}\delta_{\alpha_{1}\phantom{{}^{\prime}}\alpha_{2}\dots\alpha_{m}}^{\alpha^{\prime}_{1}\alpha^{\prime}_{2}\dots\alpha^{\prime}_{m}}\,,

(143)

with the ordered reference indices
\alpha^{\prime}_{1}<\alpha^{\prime}_{2}<\cdots<\alpha^{\prime}_{m}
.

Properties of Gaussian states in
\mathcal{B}_{2}
.

As the case of
m=2
is of particular interest in the subsequent section, we explicitly find the following
2n\times 2n
frame coefficient matrix in a
2\times 2
block-diagonal form

\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2d}}J\,,\qquad J=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\,,

(144)

for
J
the symplectic identity.
Notice that each
i
-th block is associated with the Pauli-Z on the
i
-th qubit (cf. second sum in the right-hand side in Eq. (
142
)) as the result of the product of two successive Majorana operators
Z_{i}=-iC_{2i-1}C_{2i}
. More precisely, the left Majorana operator in the product is indicated by the columns, while the rows indicate the right Majorana operator in the product.
This matrix
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
obeys a specific standard form for anti-symmetric matrices, i.e., any anti-symmetric matrix of even dimension can be brought into this
2\times 2
block-diagonal matrix under a special orthogonal transformation. Therefore, the eigenvalues of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
are
\pm i/\sqrt{2d}
according to the spectral theorem and the special form of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
, which directly allows for reading off the eigenvalues from the off-diagonal values in the blocks. Notably, all eigenvalues are of magnitude
1/\sqrt{2d}
.
Transitioning from the all-zero state to any Gaussian state of even parity, which are related by a matchgate (free-fermionic) transformation. Through the matchgate representation
R
, the effect of this transformation is reflected in a special orthogonal transformation of the anti-symmetric coefficient matrix
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
. Hence, the coefficient matrix
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}
for any Gaussian state is equivalent to the matrix
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
of the all-zero state under a special orthogonal transformation and, hence, has the same eigenvalues.

This relation allows one to characterize the Hilbert-Schmidt overlap
\braket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{j},\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{k}}_{\rm HS}
between the
m=2
matrices of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{j},\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{k}
of two even-parity Gaussian states

\ket{\psi_{j}}=U_{j}\ket{0}^{\otimes n}\,,\qquad\ket{\psi_{k}}=U_{k}\ket{0}^{\otimes n}.

(145)

Let
Q_{j},Q_{k}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
denote the corresponding orthogonal transformations acting over the Majorana indices, namely

U_{j}^{\dagger}C_{\alpha}U_{j}=\sum_{\beta=1}^{2n}[Q_{j}]_{\alpha\beta}C_{\beta}\,,\qquad U_{k}^{\dagger}C_{\alpha}U_{k}=\sum_{\beta=1}^{2n}[Q_{k}]_{\alpha\beta}C_{\beta}\,.

(146)

Then their coefficient matrices satisfy

\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{j}=Q_{j}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}Q_{j}^{\top}\,,\qquad\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{k}=Q_{k}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}Q_{k}^{\top}\,.

(147)

Now, introducing the relative rotation

R_{jk}=Q_{j}^{\top}Q_{k}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)\,,

(148)

the Hilbert-Schmidt product can be written as

\displaystyle\braket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{j},\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{k}}_{\rm HS}

\displaystyle={\rm Tr}[\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{j}^{\top}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{k}]

(149)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2d}{\rm Tr}\left[J^{\top}R_{jk}JR_{jk}^{\top}\right]

(150)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2d}{\rm Tr}\left[JR_{jk}JR_{jk}^{\top}\right]\,.

(151)

with
J
as defined in Eq. (
144
).
Thus, the overlap depends only on the relative orthogonal transformation
R_{jk}
. Now, for completely random states
\ket{\psi_{j}},\ket{\psi_{k}}
, we can only derive the trivial upper bound via the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

\left|\braket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{j},\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{k}}_{\rm HS}\right|\leq\left\lVert\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{j}\right\rVert_{\rm HS}\left\lVert\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{k}\right\rVert_{\rm HS}=\frac{n}{d}\,.

(152)

However, we can derive average bounds by leveraging the orthogonal nature of
R_{jk}
. Indeed, if
U_{j}
and
U_{k}
are independently Haar-random in
\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)
, then
R_{jk}
is Haar-random in
\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
, and the matrix

S=R_{jk}JR_{jk}^{\top}

(153)

is a random orthogonal complex structure. By
\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
-invariance and anti-symmetry, when averaging over the random
R_{jk}
its second moments take the form

\mathbb{E}\!\left[S_{\mu\nu}S_{\alpha\beta}\right]=\frac{1}{2n-1}\left(\delta_{\mu\alpha}\delta_{\nu\beta}-\delta_{\mu\beta}\delta_{\nu\alpha}\right)\,,

(154)

where the prefactor is fixed by the identity

\braket{S,S}_{\rm HS}=\braket{J,J}_{\rm HS}=2n\,.

(155)

It then follows that

\mathbb{E}\!\left[\braket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{j},\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{k}}_{\rm HS}\right]=0\,,

(156)

and

\displaystyle{\rm Var}\!\left[\braket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{j},\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{k}}_{\rm HS}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\!\left[\braket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{j},\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{k}}_{\rm HS}^{2}\right]

(157)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4d^{2}}\,\mathbb{E}\!\left[\braket{J,S}_{\rm HS}^{2}\right]

(158)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4d^{2}}\,\frac{4n}{2n-1}

(159)

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{(2n-1)d^{2}}\in\Theta\left(\frac{1}{d^{2}}\right)

(160)

Hence, for two independent Haar-random even-parity Gaussian states, one finds the large-
n
upper and lower bounds

\mathrm{Std}\left[\braket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{j},\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{k}}_{\rm HS}\right]\in\Theta\left(\frac{1}{d}\right)\,,

(161)

where
{\rm Std}[\cdot]
denotes the standard deviation, meaning that the typical size of the inner product between the coefficient matrices of two random Gaussian states is
1/d
.
In other words, although all Gaussian states have the same norm in
\mathcal{B}_{2}
, two generic such states become exponentially orthogonal in the large-
n
limit.

C.1.2
Pauli observables

As previously mentioned, Pauli observables are not only fully contained in a single module
\mathcal{B}_{m}
but are also equivalent to a single element
C_{\mathcal{M}}
of the module basis defined in Eq. (
58
) up to a phase. Let
\mathcal{M}=\{\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{m}\}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,2n\}^{m}
denote the index set of the
m
distinct Majorana operators composing the product associated with the Pauli observable. Similarly, the individual (
m!
many) elements in the module frame (Eq. (
59
)), corresponding to permutations of the Majorana products of this module basis element, are equally equivalent to the Pauli observable.
Therefore, the coefficient in the tensors
\bm{O}
of the projection of a Pauli observable
\hat{O}
onto the module
\mathcal{B}_{m}
only exhibit
m!
many non-zero components corresponding to the
m
distinct Majorana operators referenced by the index set
\mathcal{M}
.
Formally, with fixing the order
\mu_{1}<\mu_{2}\ldots<\mu_{m}
in the unique index vector
\bm{\mu}
for the Pauli observable, the only non-zero elements are

o_{\pi(\bm{\mu})}=\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\sqrt{\frac{d}{m!}}

(162)

for all index permutations
\pi(\bm{\mu})
with
\pi\in S_{m}
where
\mathrm{sgn}(\pi)\in\{-1,1\}
denotes the sign of the permutation
\pi
.
Importantly, this precisely recovers the (rank-
m
) generalized (anti-symmetric) Kronecker delta tensor for the non-zero part

o_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\ldots\mu_{m}}=\sqrt{\frac{d}{m!}}\delta_{\mu_{1}\phantom{{}^{\prime}}\mu_{2}\dots\mu_{m}}^{\mu^{\prime}_{1}\mu^{\prime}_{2}\dots\mu^{\prime}_{m}}\,,

(163)

with the ordered reference indices
\mu^{\prime}_{1}<\mu^{\prime}_{2}<\cdots<\mu^{\prime}_{m}
.
Any other choice of indices
\bm{\nu}
, which is not equivalent to
\bm{\mu}
up to permutation, results in a zero component
o_{\bm{\nu}}=0
. Effectively, the rank-
m
frame coefficient tensors
\bm{O}
for a Pauli observable reduce from size
2n
to size
m
per dimension.

C.2
Special GP families in
\mathcal{B}_{2}

In this section, we classify various families of observables, states, and their combinations based on whether they induce GPs within the
\mathcal{B}_{2}
module.

C.2.1
GPs from Gaussian states in
\mathcal{B}_{2}

In the univariate case of a single (pure) Gaussian state
\rho
, checking Condition
2
, which was simplified for
\mathcal{B}_{2}
to a condition on fractions of norms of
{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}=\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{\top}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}
, as stated in Eq. (
133
) of Proposition
B.5
, becomes trivial.
Since Gaussian states lead to the special form as defined in Eq. (
144
), we use
{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}=\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{\top}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}=-\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{2}=1/(d\cdot m!)\openone
. Hence, the eigenvalues of
{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}
are all
1/(d\cdot m!)
as the negative squares of the purely imaginary eigenvalues
\pm i/\sqrt{d\cdot m!}
of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}
. Because the norm ratio is scale-invariant, the norms of the identity differ by a factor of
n
:

\frac{\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\rVert_{1}^{2}}=\frac{\lVert\openone\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\lVert\openone\rVert_{1}^{2}}=\frac{2n}{\left(2n\right)^{2}}=\frac{1}{2n}\in\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\subset o(1).

(164)

Since further, for any observable
O\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
, the corresponding norm fraction
{\lVert\mathbb{O}\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\lVert\mathbb{O}\rVert_{1}^{2}}\leq 1
, Condition
2
is fulfilled. The remaining Condition
1
is trivially fulfilled by the assumption of an arbitrary observable
O
in the module
\mathcal{B}_{2}
. Therefore, Theorem
1
holds, and matchgate transformations induce univariate
gp
from any Gaussian state and any observable
O\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
.

The univariate case is clearly special, since Proposition
B.5
reduces Condition
2
to the asymptotic vanishing of a ratio of Schatten norms of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}=\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{\top}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}
, which we have easily shown to always hold. In the multivariate case, however, the relevant quantity is no longer a single positive ratio, but rather the full sum
S_{\mathrm{pair}}
of pairwise matching contributions appearing in Eq. (
104
). This makes it harder in general to check for the emergence of a provable GP. In the following however, we show that for a dataset of positively correlated Gaussian states one can derive a condition on the covariances that guarantees the satisfaction of Condition
2
.

Proposition C.1

(A multivariate QGP in
\mathcal{B}_{2}
)
.

Given a dataset of even-parity Gaussian states
\rho_{i}
, and an observable
O\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
, the condition

\Braket{\rho_{i},\rho_{j}}_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}\in\omega\left(\frac{n^{2/3}}{d}\right)\qquad\forall\,i\neq j\,\,

(165)

is enough to satisfy Condition
2
and hence ensure the emergence of a multivariate GP.

Proof.

Let us first recall that, for an observable
O\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
we are guaranteed that the ratios of observable frame-coefficient contractions are at most 1 (see Schatten norm equivalence and note in Proposition
C.2
), and hence to prove the emergence of a multivariate GP we can simply study the asymptotics of the states’ contributions.
Now, let
\rho_{i}
be even-parity Gaussian states, and let
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i)}
be their corresponding
2n\times 2n
frame-coefficient matrix. One readily finds that
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i)}=Q_{i}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}Q_{i}^{\top}
, for
Q_{i}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{SO}}(2n)
and
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2d}}J
, with
J
being the symplectic identity. Hence, all
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i)}
share the same eigenvalues as
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
, which have magnitude
1/\sqrt{2d}
. In particular, we have

\|\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i)}\|_{\infty}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2d}}\,.

(166)

Now consider, for any even
k\geq 4
moment, a non-pairwise contraction appearing in
S_{\neg\mathrm{pair}}
in Condition
2
, associated to the Brauer pairing
\sigma
. Since the frame-coefficient tensors in
\mathcal{B}_{2}
are anti-symmetric matrices
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j})}
, the only Brauer pairings
\sigma
yielding non-automatically vanishing contractions due to self-loops are those that can be interpreted as products of cycles between the
k
frame-coefficient matrices involved. Let us denote by
{\rm cycles}(\sigma)
the set of such cycles, by
\mathcal{C}
one such cycle, and by
\ell_{\mathcal{C}}
the length of
\mathcal{C}
. The Brauer pairing
\sigma
further contains permutations of the indices of the indices of each individual frame-coefficient matrix. However, the latter can only induce transpositions of the
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j})}
, that is a sign since they are anti-symmetric. On the other hand, each cycle
\mathcal{C}\in{\rm cycles}(\sigma)
contributes a trace of the product of the frame-coefficient matrices picked up by
\mathcal{C}
.
For later convenience, let us split the cycles of
\sigma
into those of length
2
, whose set we denote by
\mathrm{cycles}_{2}(\sigma)\subset{\rm cycles}(\sigma)
, and those of length strictly larger than
2
, whose set we similarly denote by
\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)\subseteq{\rm cycles}(\sigma)
.
Let us denote by
|\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)|
the number of the latter appearing in
{\rm cycles}(\sigma)
. Note that since by definition
\sigma
is non-pairwise it must be that
|\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)|\geq 1
.
The full state contribution magnitude coming from
\sigma
then factorizes as

\left\lvert\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}\!\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j})}\right)\right\rvert=\left\lvert\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\mathrm{cycles}_{2}(\sigma)}\Braket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j})},\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j^{\prime}})}}_{\rm HS}\cdot\prod_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)}\left({\rm Tr}\!\left[\prod_{i_{j}\in\mathcal{C}}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j})}\right]\right)\right\rvert\,.

(167)

For each cycle
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)
, the corresponding state contribution satisfies

\left|{\rm Tr}\!\left[\prod_{i_{j}\in\mathcal{C}}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j})}\right]\right|\leq\operatorname{rank}\!\left(\prod_{i_{j}\in\mathcal{C}}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j})}\right)\left\|\prod_{i_{j}\in\mathcal{C}}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j})}\right\|_{\infty}\leq 2n\prod_{i_{j}\in\mathcal{C}}\|\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j})}\|_{\infty}=\frac{2n}{(2d)^{\ell_{\mathcal{C}}/2}}\,.

(168)

Using this we can bound the full state contribution associated to
\sigma
by

\left|\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}\!\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j})}\right)\right|\leq\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\mathrm{cycles}_{2}(\sigma)}\Braket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j})},\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j^{\prime}})}}_{\rm HS}\frac{(2n)^{|\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)|}}{(2d)^{\sum_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)}\ell_{\mathcal{C}}/2}}\,.

(169)

On the other hand, each state contribution term in
S_{\mathrm{pair}}
associated to a pairwise matching
\bm{\sigma}
is of the form

\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}}\Braket{\rho_{i_{j}},\rho_{i_{j^{\prime}}}}_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}=\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}}\Braket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j})},\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j^{\prime}})}}_{\rm HS}\,.

(170)

We now use the bound from Eq. (
169
) to study the asymptotics of the ratio

\frac{\left|\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}\!\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{k}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j})}\right)\right|}{\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}}\Braket{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j})},\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{(i_{j^{\prime}})}}_{\rm HS}}\,,

(171)

Let us start by assuming that all pairwise module overlaps are non-negative, so as to avoid cancellations in
S_{\mathrm{pair}}
and allows us to analyze the ratios in Eq. (
171
) independently as per Proposition
B.3
, sufficient to fulfill Condition
2
. We then compare a non-pairwise contribution with a pairwise matching contribution associated to some
\bm{\sigma}
chosen so as to contain all the
2
-cycles already appearing in
\sigma
as Proposition
B.3
requires only the existence of such a pairwise matching
\bm{\sigma}
. In this way, the pairwise factors already present in Eq. (
169
) also appear in the chosen denominator in Eq. (
171
) and can be canceled out.
Now, writing

\Braket{\rho_{i},\rho_{j}}_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}=\frac{n}{d}\,r_{ij},

(172)

where
n/d
is the largest possible module overlap inside
\mathcal{B}_{2}

Deneris et al. 2025
and
0\leq r_{ij}\leq 1
denotes the corresponding normalized overlap, the remaining pairwise matching contribution reads

\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}\setminus\mathrm{cycles}_{2}(\sigma)}\Braket{\rho_{i_{j}},\rho_{i_{j^{\prime}}}}_{\mathcal{B}_{2}}=\left(\frac{n}{d}\right)^{k/2-|\mathrm{cycles}_{2}(\sigma)|}\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}\setminus\mathrm{cycles}_{2}(\sigma)}r_{i_{j}i_{j^{\prime}}}\,.

(173)

Note that
k/2-|\mathrm{cycles}_{2}(\sigma)|=\sum_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)}\ell_{\mathcal{C}}/2
.
For the corresponding pairwise matching term to dominate the non-pairwise contribution, namely, the ratio in Eq. (
171
) being
o(1)
, the strict asymptotic lower bound

\prod_{(j,j^{\prime})\in\bm{\sigma}\setminus\mathrm{cycles}_{2}(\sigma)}r_{i_{j}i_{j^{\prime}}}\in\omega\left((2n)^{|\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)|-\sum_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)}\ell_{\mathcal{C}}/2}\right)\,

(174)

must hold.
If we now further impose a uniform lower bound on the normalized overlaps, namely

r_{ij}\geq r_{*}(n)\qquad\forall\,i\neq j,

(175)

then a sufficient condition is obtained by requiring

r_{*}(n)^{\sum_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)}\ell_{\mathcal{C}}/2}\in\omega\left(n^{|\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)|-\sum_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)}\ell_{\mathcal{C}}/2}\right)\,.

(176)

Equivalently, this reads

r_{*}(n)\in\omega\left(n^{\frac{2|\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)|}{\sum_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)}\ell_{\mathcal{C}}}-1}\right)\,.

(177)

Now, since every cycle in
\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)
has length at least
3
, one has

\sum_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)}\ell_{\mathcal{C}}\geq 3|\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)|\,,

(178)

and thus

\frac{2|\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)|}{\sum_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{cycles}_{>2}(\sigma)}\ell_{\mathcal{C}}}-1\leq-\frac{1}{3}\,.

(179)

Hence, a sufficient condition on the individual normalized overlaps is the following strict asymptotic lower bound

r_{ij}\in\omega\left(n^{-1/3}\right)\qquad\forall\,i\neq j\,.

(180)

Thus, assuming non-negative module overlaps, it is sufficient that all normalized overlaps
r_{ij}
decay more slowly than
n^{-1/3}
in order for the pairwise matching contributions to dominate the non-pairwise ones. Since as we discussed in the beginning, we are guaranteed that observable contributions are at most 1, this overall fulfills the sufficient formulation of Condition
2
via Proposition
B.3
by satisfying Eq. (
129
) and trivially Eq. (
130
). Consequently, with ensured zero odd moments in
\mathcal{B}_{2}
(Corollary
B.3
), a set of Gaussian states with bounded module overlaps as in Eq. (
180
), which is the statement of Proposition
C.1
once the normalization factor
n/d
is reinserted, is guaranteed to form multivariate QGPs for any observable
O\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
.
∎

C.2.2
Pauli observables (in
\mathcal{B}_{2}
) alone cannot lead to GPs

Since any Pauli observable is fully contained in only a single module (see Sec.
C.1.2
), Condition
1
is trivially fulfilled.
As we are furthermore considering the
m=2
Majorana case, Proposition
B.5
provides an equivalent simplification for Condition
2
such that it suffices to check the scaling of the Schatten norm fractions

{\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{2}}/{\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{1}}
associated with the observable.

Here,
\mathbb{O}=O^{\top}O
where
O
denotes the matrix
2n\times 2n
reflecting the rank-
2
tensors
\bm{O}
of the frame coefficients when projecting the observable
\hat{O}
onto the module
\mathcal{B}_{2}
.

To demonstrate that Pauli observables alone cannot lead to GPs, we show that these fractions remain constant in scaling
n
, which leaves the strict asymptotic vanishing (Condition
2
) to the specific choice of states.
As highlighted in Sec.
C.1.2
, considering Pauli observables reduces to the study of a single rank-
m
generalized (anti-symmetric) Kronecker delta tensor up to a factor.
Hence, reshaped into a
2\times 2
matrix, we uncover the
2\times 2
identity as

\mathbb{O}=O^{\top}O=\sqrt{\frac{d}{m!}}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{pmatrix}\sqrt{\frac{d}{m!}}\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}={\frac{d}{m!}}\openone.

(181)

Then, we simply find the two norms as

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left({\frac{d}{m!}}\right)^{2}\left\lVert\openone\right\rVert_{1}^{2}=\left({\frac{d}{m!}}\right)^{2}(m!)^{2}={d}^{2},

(182)

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle={\frac{d}{2}}\left\lVert\openone\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\left({\frac{d}{m!}}\right)^{2}m!={\frac{d^{2}}{m!}}.

(183)

Their ratio is constant

\frac{\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{2}}{\left\lVert\mathbb{O}\right\rVert_{1}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m!}}\in\Theta(1)\nsubseteq o(1),

(184)

i.e., does not strictly vanish asymptotically as functions in
o(1)
,
since
m=2
does not scale with
n
.

The scaling of the Pauli observable norm fractions cannot meet Eq. (
133
) in Proposition
B.5
(equivalent simplification of Condition
2
).

Hence, the statement transfers that a Pauli observable alone is not sufficient to obtain a GP, and a state family must be chosen to satisfy Condition
2
. Proposition
B.5
again provides a simplified condition in terms of Schatten norm ratios of the state module coefficients for the univariate case.

C.2.3
GPs from random two Majorana product observables

We study how inside the module
\mathcal{B}_{2}
, a random observable can yield a Gaussian process without imposing strong conditions on the dataset of states. In Supp. Fig.
C.1
we show the numerical analysis that led us to prove that the ratios
(||\mathbb{O}||_{\ell}/||\mathbb{O}||_{1})^{\ell}
scale as
\mathcal{O}(1/(2n)^{\ell-1})
in the large-
n
limit.

Supplemental Figure C.1:
Numerical verification of asymptotic GP conditions satisfaction.
Per Theorem
C.2
, an observable
\hat{O}\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
with random i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
coefficients shows vanishing coefficient tensor norm ratios
(||O||_{\ell}/||O||_{1})^{\ell}
towards
\mathrm{Cat}(\ell)/(2n)^{\ell-1}
, guaranteeing GPs in the large-
n
limit.
The solid lines and associated shaded regions represent the mean and standard deviation of
(||O||_{\ell}/||O||_{1})^{\ell}(2n)^{\ell-1}
in log-space over
100
samples of
\hat{O}
. The dashed lines instead represent the theoretically predicted convergence value
{\rm Cat}(\ell)
. The colors correspond to different values of
\ell
, as stated in the legend.

We consider observables
\hat{O}=-i\sum_{\mu,\nu}o_{\mu\nu}\,C_{\mu}C_{\nu}
where the coefficients
o_{\mu,\nu}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
are sampled i.i.d. from a Gaussian distribution of zero mean and unit variance. For increasing system sizes
n=1,\dots,100
we sample
100
independent observables
\hat{O}
and use their
(2n\times 2n)
coefficient matrices
O
and construct
\mathbb{O}=O^{\top}O
. We then compute the random variable
(||\mathbb{O}||_{\ell}/||\mathbb{O}||_{1})^{\ell}
for
\ell=2,4,\dots,10
and finally compute its mean and standard deviation. Supp. Fig.
C.1
clearly shows that after an initial decaying phase at small
n
, these ratios converge to an exact multiple of
(2n)^{\ell-1}
, and in the following we prove why.

Proposition C.2
.

Let
O\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
be a random observable with i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
coefficients in the
\mathcal{B}_{2}
basis, defined in Eq. (
58
).
In the large-
n
limit, the ratio
(||\mathbb{O}||_{\ell}/||\mathbb{O}||_{1})^{\ell}
converges almost surely
9
9

9

That is, with overwhelming probability over the sampling of the random operator
O
.
to

(||\mathbb{O}||_{\ell}/||\mathbb{O}||_{1})^{\ell}\longrightarrow\frac{\rm{Cat}(\ell)}{(2n)^{\ell-1}}\,,

(185)

where
\rm{Cat}(\ell)=\frac{1}{\ell+1}\binom{2\ell}{\ell}
is the
\ell
-th Catalan number and constant in
n
.

Proof.

Let us recall that
\mathbb{O}=O^{\top}O
for
O
the random anti-symmetric
2n\times 2n
matrix whose independent entries are sampled i.i.d. from
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.
Let us now define the Hermitian matrix

H=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2n}}O\,.

(186)

Notice that by construction the diagonal entries of
H
vanish, while its off-diagonal entries have mean zero and variance
1/2n
, so
H
is a Wigner-type matrix.

In terms of
H
, the ratios can be readily found to read

(||\mathbb{O}||_{\ell}/||\mathbb{O}||_{1})^{\ell}=\frac{1}{(2n)^{\ell-1}}\frac{\frac{1}{2n}\mathrm{Tr}(H^{2\ell})}{\left(\frac{1}{2n}\mathrm{Tr}(H^{2})\right)^{\ell}}\,.

(187)

We explicitly factorized out the
(2n)^{\ell-1}
factor, this way the whole problem has reduced to understanding the normalized traces
\frac{1}{2n}\mathrm{Tr}(H^{2\ell})
as
n\to\infty
.

Now, let
\lambda_{1}^{(n)},\dots,\lambda_{2n}^{(n)}
be the eigenvalues of
H
:

\frac{1}{2n}\mathrm{Tr}(H^{2\ell})=\frac{1}{2n}\sum_{j=1}^{2n}\left(\lambda_{j}^{(n)}\right)^{2\ell}\,.

(188)

By the Wigner semicircle law, the eigenvalue distribution of
H
converges, in the large-
n
limit, almost surely to the semicircle density

\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{4-x^{2}}\,\mathbf{1}_{[-2,2]}(x)\,,

(189)

for
\mathbf{1}_{[-2,2]}(x)
the indicator function on the interval
[-2,2]
, namely the function

\mathbf{1}_{[-2,2]}(x)=\begin{cases}1\quad&{\rm if}\,x\in[-2,2]\\
0&{\rm else}\end{cases}\,.

(190)

Hence, for any integer
\ell\geq 1
,

\frac{1}{2n}\mathrm{Tr}(H^{2\ell})\underset{\rm a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\int_{-2}^{2}x^{2\ell}\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)\,dx\,.

(191)

Using this, we see that all we need are the even moments of the semicircle law.

Let us denote the latter as

\mu_{2\ell}=\int_{-2}^{2}x^{2\ell}\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(x)\,dx=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-2}^{2}x^{2\ell}\sqrt{4-x^{2}}\,dx\,.

(192)

We can use the fact that the integrand is even to write

\mu_{2\ell}=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{0}^{2}x^{2\ell}\sqrt{4-x^{2}}\,dx\,.

(193)

Now let us perform the change of variable
x=2\sqrt{t}
, this leads to

\mu_{2\ell}=\frac{2^{2\ell+1}}{\pi}\int_{0}^{1}t^{\ell-\frac{1}{2}}(1-t)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,dt\,.

(194)

One can then recognize in the expression above the Beta function,

\mu_{2\ell}=\frac{2^{2\ell+1}}{\pi}B\left(\ell+\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2}\right)\,.

(195)

Then, using
B(a,b)=\frac{\Gamma(a)\Gamma(b)}{\Gamma(a+b)}
and
\Gamma(3/2)=\sqrt{\pi}/2
, we obtain

\mu_{2\ell}=\frac{2^{2\ell}}{\sqrt{\pi}}\frac{\Gamma\left(\ell+\frac{1}{2}\right)}{\Gamma(\ell+2)}\,.

(196)

Lastly, by using

\Gamma\left(\ell+\frac{1}{2}\right)=\frac{(2\ell)!}{4^{\ell}\ell!}\sqrt{\pi}\quad\text{and}\quad\Gamma\left(\ell+2\right)=\left(\ell+1\right)!,

(197)

we find

\mu_{2\ell}=\frac{(2\ell)!}{\ell!(\ell+1)!}=\frac{1}{\ell+1}\binom{2\ell}{\ell}=\mathrm{Cat}(\ell)\,.

(198)

Plugging this back in Eq. (
187
), we conclude the proof.

Finally, we note that fluctuations over the convergence scale as
Anderson et al. 2010

(||\mathbb{O}||_{\ell}/||\mathbb{O}||_{1})^{\ell}\sim\frac{\rm{Cat}(\ell)}{(2n)^{\ell-1}}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n^{l}}\right)\,,

(199)

which implies through a standard upper tail bound that probability of deviation decays exponentially with
n
.

∎

In conclusion, the vanishing Schatten norm ratio in Eq. (
185
) for the case
\ell=2
suffices already to guarantee that Condition
2
is met via Proposition
B.5
in the univariate case. Therefore, GPs emerge in the large-
n
limit with overwhelming probability when sampling such random
\mathcal{B}_{2}
observables
\hat{O}

C.2.4
GPs from magic states in
\mathcal{B}_{2}

Consider now the family of magic states, known as the extent state
Diaz et al. 2023
,

\ket{\psi_{\theta}}=\left(\cos\left(\frac{\theta}{4}\right)\ket{0000}+\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{4}\right)\ket{1111}\right)^{\otimes n/4}\,,

(200)

for
n
divisible by
4
, and let
\rho_{\theta}=\ket{\psi_{\theta}}\!\bra{\psi_{\theta}}
. In the univariate case of a single such state, checking Condition
2
for the module
\mathcal{B}_{2}
is again immediate via Proposition
B.5
. Indeed, by the module frame conventions, the entries of the two-Majorana coefficient matrix
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}
are

\rho_{\alpha\beta}={\rm Tr}\left[C_{\alpha\beta}\rho_{\theta}\right]=\frac{-i}{\sqrt{2d}}{\rm Tr}\left[C_{\alpha}C_{\beta}\rho_{\theta}\right]\,.

(201)

Hence, it suffices to characterize the two-point Majorana correlators of
\rho_{\theta}
.

Let us first consider a single four-qubit block

\ket{\phi_{\theta}}=\cos\left(\frac{\theta}{4}\right)\ket{0000}+\sin\left(\frac{\theta}{4}\right)\ket{1111}\,.

(202)

In the Jordan-Wigner representation, for each qubit
q
in the block one has

C_{2q-1}C_{2q}=iZ_{q}\,,

(203)

and therefore

\bra{\phi_{\theta}}C_{2q-1}C_{2q}\ket{\phi_{\theta}}=i\bra{\phi_{\theta}}Z_{q}\ket{\phi_{\theta}}=i\left(\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\theta}{4}\right)-\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\theta}{4}\right)\right)=i\cos\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)\,.

(204)

On the other hand, if the two Majorana operators act on different qubits, then the corresponding Pauli string contains two
X/Y
terms and therefore it maps both
\ket{0000}
and
\ket{1111}
to computational basis states that are orthogonal to each other. Thus, all such matrix elements vanish. Since the full state
\ket{\psi_{\theta}}
is a tensor product of identical four-qubit blocks, the same argument also implies that all mixed two-point functions between Majoranas belonging to different blocks vanish. It follows that the full two-Majorana coefficient matrix is of the form

\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}=\frac{\cos\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)}{\sqrt{2d}}\,J\,,

(205)

where
J
is the standard symplectic form on
2n
Majorana modes as defined in Eq. (
144
). Consequently,

\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}=\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{\top}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}=\frac{\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)}{2d}\,\openone=\frac{\cos^{2}\left(\frac{\theta}{2}\right)}{d\cdot 2!}\,\openone\,.

(206)

Therefore, for all
\theta
such that
\cos(\theta/2)\neq 0
, the eigenvalues of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}
are all equal and we obtain

\frac{\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\rVert_{1}^{2}}=\frac{\lVert\openone\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\lVert\openone\rVert_{1}^{2}}=\frac{2n}{\left(2n\right)^{2}}=\frac{1}{2n}\in\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\subset o(1)\,.

(207)

Since further, for any observable
O\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
, the corresponding norm fraction
{\lVert\mathbb{O}\rVert_{2}^{2}}/{\lVert\mathbb{O}\rVert_{1}^{2}}\leq 1
, Condition
2
is fulfilled. The remaining Condition
1
is trivially fulfilled by the assumption of an arbitrary observable
O
in the module
\mathcal{B}_{2}
. Therefore, Theorem
1
holds also for the family of product states
\rho_{\theta}
, for all
\theta
such that
\cos(\theta/2)\neq 0
, and Haar-random matchgate unitaries induce univariate
gp
from these states and any observable
O\in\mathcal{B}_{2}
.

In Supp. Fig.
C.2
we show the empirical GP distribution for magic states
\rho_{\theta}
for different values of
\theta
and a random Pauli measurement in
\mathcal{B}_{2}
, together with the expected Gaussian curve, which using Eq. (
138
) and Eq. (
206
) can be readily found to be
\mathcal{N}(0,|\cos(\theta/2)|/\sqrt{2n-1})
.

Supplemental Figure C.2:
Numerical verification of a univariate GP for magic states and Pauli observable in
\mathcal{B}_{2}
.
We consider a system of
n=12
qubits. For each of the seven equispaced values of
\theta\in[0,2\pi/3]
, we take
5\cdot 10^{4}
samples of the random variable given by the expectation value of
O=Z_{1}
over the state
\sigma_{\theta}=U\rho_{\theta}U^{\dagger}
, with
U\sim\operatorname{\mathbb{SPIN}}(2n)
. The blue histograms show the numerical distribution of the samples, while the orange curves represent the expected Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,|\cos(\theta/2)|/\sqrt{2n-1})
. Different shades of the colors correspond to different values of
\theta
, increasing from lighter to darker. Note that while a
\mathcal{B}_{2}
Pauli observable alone is insufficient to induce a GP, the magic state
\rho_{\theta}
successfully drives the asymptotic convergence.

C.3
Special GP families in
\mathcal{B}_{4}

In this section, we analyze various families of states and observables for the module
\mathcal{B}_{4}
with respect to their suitability for forming GPs. A particular focus is on Pauli observables, which alone cannot lead to GPs in
\mathcal{B}_{4}
. More interestingly, we identify that, when combined with Gaussian states, they can never lead to GPs, while providing numerical evidence that a class of random states can help to achieve GPs with
\mathcal{B}_{4}
Pauli observables.

C.3.1
Gaussian states alone cannot lead to GPs in
\mathcal{B}_{4}

The following analysis of the behavior of Gaussian states when
O\in\mathcal{B}_{4}
relies on a particular subset of ratios in Eq. (
125
) of the equivalent univariate formulation of Proposition
B.2
that are analogous to the simplified
\mathcal{B}_{2}
version of Condition
2
in Eq. (
133
) of Proposition
B.5
. Specifically, these are ratios that correspond to Schatten norm comparisons. Since these ratios naturally occur as a subset of the full condition for
\mathcal{B}_{4}
(Proposition
B.2
), we prove that Condition
2
cannot be fulfilled, provided that the ratio associated with the observable does not vanish sufficiently fast.
Particularly, we study the cases of Proposition
B.2
(simplified
\mathcal{B}_{2}
condition) where the contraction over four replications of the (rank-4) frame coefficient tensor
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}
of a Gaussian state arranges them on a cycle where each tensor is connected to two other tensors via two (index) legs each. As in Proposition
B.5
, the state coefficient tensor
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bm{\uprho}$}}}}
can then be reshaped into square matrices
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}
and their contractions (magnitudes) are expressed as the (absolute) trace of matrix powers
\left\lvert{\rm Tr}\left[\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{4}\right]\right\rvert
in the numerator and
\left\lvert{\rm Tr}\left[\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{2}\right]\right\rvert
in the denominator. Furthermore, the relation to Schatten 2- and 1-norms then still holds as
\left\lvert{\rm Tr}\left[\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{4}\right]\right\rvert=\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}
and
\left\lvert{\rm Tr}\left[\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{2}\right]\right\rvert=\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\right\rVert_{1}
, respectively. Any Gaussian state with non-zero support in
\mathcal{B}_{4}
(which implies even parity) can be prepared by a matchgate transformation applied to the all-zero state
\ket{0}^{\otimes n}
, which is related to a special orthogonal transformation of the all-zero state
\mathcal{B}_{4}
frame coefficient matrix
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
. As the traces/norms are invariant under such an orthogonal basis change, the ratio for any Gaussian state matches the ratio associated with the all-zero state, i.e.,

\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\right\rVert_{2}/\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}\right\rVert_{1}=\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\right\rVert_{2}/\left\lVert{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\right\rVert_{1}

with
{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}=\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{\top}_{0}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
.
Therefore, studying the asymptotic behavior of this ratio for the all-zero state allows for drawing conclusions about all Gaussian states. Hence, we provide a partial violation of Condition
2
for any Gaussian state in the
\mathcal{B}_{4}
case. As long as this scaling is not counteracted by the ratio with respect to the observable
O\in\mathcal{B}_{4}
to yield an asymptotic vanishing as required by Condition
2
, a Gaussian process cannot be formed as per Theorem
2
.

The following Theorem shows that when Gaussian states are considered, the ratio
\frac{\|{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[3.01389pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\|_{2}}{\|{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[3.01389pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\|_{1}}
converges to a constant in the large-
n
limit.

Proposition C.3
.

The ratio of the Schatten 2 and 1-norms of the squared, reshaped tensor of coefficients
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
of the reference Gaussian state
\ket{0}^{\otimes n}
, i.e.,
{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}=\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}^{\top}_{0}\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
is

\left(\frac{\|{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\|_{2}}{\|{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\|_{1}}\right)^{2}=\frac{n^{2}-3n+5}{9n(n-1)}\,.

(208)

In the large-
n
limit, it hence holds

\left(\frac{\|{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\|_{2}}{\|{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\|_{1}}\right)^{2}\longrightarrow\frac{1}{9}\,.

(209)

Proof.

By direct computation, one readily finds

[\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}]_{(ij),(kl)}=J_{ij}J_{kl}-J_{ik}J_{jl}+J_{il}J_{jk}\,,

(210)

from elements of the symplectic form
J\in\mathbb{R}^{2n\times 2n}
as defined in Eq. (
144
).
By grouping arbitrary index pairs
(i,j)
and
(k,l)
, we have constructed
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
as a matrix of size
(2n)^{2}\times(2n)^{2}
. However, since
[\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}]_{(ij),(kl)}
is anti-symmetric under
i\leftrightarrow j
and under
k\leftrightarrow l
, it annihilates the symmetric sector of
(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{2}
and acts non-trivially only on the anti-symmetric one. Therefore, without loss of generality, we can restrict
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
to the space of
2
-forms
10
10

10

We recall that in general, a
k
-form is a completely anti-symmetric
k
-linear map acting on
k
copies of a vector space.

\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, whose dimension is

\dim(\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2n})=\binom{2n}{2}=n(2n-1)\,.

(211)

Then, by the symmetry of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}

{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}=\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}^{2}\,.

(212)

The elements
A,B\in\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2n}
can be identified with anti-symmetric matrices
A=[A_{ij}]
and
B=[B_{ij}]
, and we can equip them with the inner product

\langle A,B\rangle=\sum_{i<j}A_{ij}B_{ij}\,.

(213)

The symplectic identity
J
is also an element of
\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, and its norm induced by the previous inner product reads

\langle J,J\rangle=n\,.

(214)

Now, we want to characterize the spectrum of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
, and hence of
{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}
, so that we compute the needed Schatten norms.
To do so, let us understand how
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
acts on an element
A
of
\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. Expanding the left product, we have

[\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}A]_{ij}=\sum_{k<l}[\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}]_{(ij),(kl)}A_{kl}=\sum_{k<l}\left(J_{ij}J_{kl}-J_{ik}J_{jl}+J_{il}J_{jk}\right)A_{kl}\,.

(215)

The first term is immediately

\sum_{k<l}J_{ij}J_{kl}A_{kl}=J_{ij}\langle J,A\rangle\,.

(216)

For the remaining two terms, using anti-symmetry of
A
and
J
, one can check that

\sum_{k<l}\bigl(-J_{ik}J_{jl}+J_{il}J_{jk}\bigr)A_{kl}=-[JAJ^{\top}]_{ij}\,.

(217)

Hence

\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}A=\langle J,A\rangle J-T(A)\quad\text{with}\quad T(A)=JAJ^{\top}\,.

(218)

The operator
T
is an involution, i.e., it is its own inverse. This can be readily seen from the fact that
J^{\top}=-J
and
J^{2}=-\openone
. This implies that
T
has eigenvalues
\pm 1
.
Now, we can decompose
\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2n}
into the eigenspaces of
T
. Using again the properties of
J
we can immediately see that
J
is an eigenvector of eigenvalue
1
of
T
. Hence the space
\mathbb{R}J
is
T
-invariant.
Let us then decompose
\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2n}
as

\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2n}=\mathbb{R}J\oplus W_{+}\oplus W_{-}\,,

(219)

where
\mathbb{R}J\oplus W_{+}
is the eigenspace of eigenvalue
+1
of
T
, with
W_{+}\perp J
, and
W_{-}
is the eigenspace of eigenvalue
-1
. We can notice that the positive eigenspace of
T
is the space of anti-symmetric matrices
A
that commute with
J
, since
JAJ^{\top}=A\to JA=AJ
using
J^{2}=-I
. Similarly, the negative eigenspace is given by the anti-symmetric matrices that anticommute with
J
. We can use this to compute the dimensions of
W_{+}
and
W_{-}
. Indeed, write
A
as

A=\begin{pmatrix}P&Q\\
-Q^{\top}&S\end{pmatrix}\,,

(220)

for
P,S
anti-symmetric
n\times n
real matrices and
Q
an
n\times n
real matrix.
Notice that in this block decomposition

J=\begin{pmatrix}0&\openone\\
-\openone&0\end{pmatrix}\,.

(221)

If we now impose
[J,A]=0
we get the condition

JA=\begin{pmatrix}-Q^{\top}&S\\
-P&-Q\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-Q&P\\
-S&-Q^{\top}\end{pmatrix}=AJ\,,

(222)

which implies
P=S
and
Q=Q^{\top}
. Hence the elements of the positive eigenspace of
J
look like

A=\begin{pmatrix}P&Q\\
-Q&P\end{pmatrix}\,,

(223)

for
P
anti-symmetric and
Q
symmetric. Since the space of
n\times n
anti-symmetric matrices has dimension
\dim(P)=\frac{n(n-1)}{2}
, while that of symmetric ones is
\dim(Q)=\frac{n(n+1)}{2}
, we readily get

\dim(W_{+})+1=\frac{n(n-1)}{2}+\frac{n(n+1)}{2}=n^{2}\,,

(224)

where we remember that we already separated the positive eigenspace of
J
as
\mathbb{R}J\oplus W_{+}
.
Since the space
\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2n}
has dimension
\dim(\Lambda^{2}\mathbb{R}^{2n})=n(2n-1)
, we get

\dim(W_{-})=n(n-1)\,.

(225)

Now let us study the action of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
on each of
\mathbb{R}J
,
W_{+}
and
W_{-}
.
First, on
J
we quickly see

\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}J=\langle J,J\rangle J-T(J)=nJ-J=(n-1)J\,.

(226)

Next, let
A\in W_{+}
. Then, since
W_{+}\perp J
,
\langle J,A\rangle=0
and
T(A)=A
, hence

\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}A=0\cdot J-A=-A\,.

(227)

Lastly, let
A\in W_{-}
. Then we again have
\langle J,A\rangle=0
and now
T(A)=-A
, so

\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}A=0\cdot J-(-A)=A\,.

(228)

From this, we get that the spectrum of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}
is

\operatorname{spec}(\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0})=\{n-1\}^{[1]}\cup\{1\}^{[n(n-1)]}\cup\{-1\}^{[n^{2}-1]}\,.

(229)

Squaring, we obtain the spectrum of
{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}=\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\uprho$}}}}_{0}^{2}

\operatorname{spec}({\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0})=\{(n-1)^{2}\}^{[1]}\cup\{1\}^{[n(n-1)]}\cup\{1\}^{[n^{2}-1]}\,.

(230)

Since
{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}
is positive semidefinite, its Schatten
1
-norm and Schatten
2
-norm satisfy

\|{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\|_{1}={\rm Tr}[{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}]\,,\qquad\|{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}={\rm Tr}[{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}^{2}]\,.

(231)

Using the spectrum above,

\|{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\|_{1}=(n-1)^{2}+\bigl(n(2n-1)-1\bigr)=3n(n-1)\,,

(232)

and

\|{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\|_{2}^{2}=(n-1)^{4}+\bigl(n(2n-1)-1\bigr)\,.

(233)

Putting all together and simplifying we arrive at

\left(\frac{\|{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\|_{2}}{\|{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\|_{1}}\right)^{2}=\frac{n^{2}-3n+5}{9n(n-1)}\,.

(234)

Lastly, taking
n\to\infty
gives

\left(\frac{\|{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\|_{2}}{\|{\mathord{\raisebox{0.0pt}[4.30554pt][0.0pt]{\resizebox{}{447828}{\raisebox{0.0pt}{$\bboldrho$}}}}}_{0}\|_{1}}\right)^{2}\longrightarrow\frac{1}{9}\,,

(235)

which concludes the proof.
∎

C.3.2
Pauli observables (in
\mathcal{B}_{4}
) alone cannot lead to GPs

Analogously to the case of Gaussian states, we here show that Pauli observables in
\mathcal{B}_{4}
cannot fulfill the equivalent univariate formulation of Condition
2
in Proposition
B.2
. Hence, this partial violation of Condition
2
can only be resolved by the state choice and either leads to GPs or prevents their formation as per Theorems
1
and
2
, respectively. Specifically, we show that some contractions yield ratios, namely the ones that form Schatten 2 vs 1-norm ratios under the observable coefficient tensor
\bm{O}
being reshaped into a matrix
O
, that do not vanish asymptotically. The following theorem proves that these kinds of ratios are constant.

Proposition C.4
.

Given a Pauli observable
\hat{O}=-C_{a}C_{b}C_{c}C_{d}\in\mathcal{B}_{4}
, let
\bm{O}
be the completely anti-symmetric tensor of its frame coefficients over
\mathcal{B}_{4}
, and
O=\bm{O}_{(ij),(kl)}
the corresponding symmetric matrix obtained by grouping the first two indices of
\bm{O}
as the row index and the last two as the column one.
Then, for
\mathbb{O}=O^{2}
we have

\left(\frac{\|\mathbb{O}\|_{2}}{\|\mathbb{O}\|_{1}}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{6}\,.

(236)

Proof.

Let us denote by
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{2n}\}
the canonical basis of
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. For any four distinct indices
a<b<c<d\in[2n]
, one has

\bm{O}=e_{a}\wedge e_{b}\wedge e_{c}\wedge e_{d}\,.

(237)

Indeed, by definition the exterior product
\wedge
returns the completely anti-symmetric combination of the four delta-like tensors associated to each Majorana index.
Then, the matrix entries of
O
read as

o_{(ij),(kl)}=[\bm{O}]_{ijkl}\,.

(238)

Notice that, grouping arbitrary pairs
(i,j)
and
(k,l)
, then
O
is a matrix of size
(2n)^{2}\times(2n)^{2}
. However, since
\bm{O}
is anti-symmetric under
i\leftrightarrow j
and under
k\leftrightarrow l
, the corresponding operator vanishes on the symmetric sector of
(\mathbb{R}^{2n})^{2}
and acts non-trivially only on the anti-symmetric one. Therefore, without loss of generality, we may restrict
O
to the space of
2
-forms
\Lambda^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, whose natural basis is given by the vectors

E_{ij}=e_{i}\wedge e_{j}\,,\qquad i<j\,.

(239)

Now, we can make the components of
O
explicit by introducing the generalized (anti-symmetric) Kronecker delta

\delta_{ij}^{pq}=\delta_{i}^{p}\delta_{j}^{q}-\delta_{i}^{q}\delta_{j}^{p}=\left(e_{i}\wedge e_{j}\right)_{p,q}\,.

(240)

From here, we have

o_{(ij),(kl)}=\delta_{ij}^{ab}\delta_{kl}^{cd}-\delta_{ij}^{ac}\delta_{kl}^{bd}+\delta_{ij}^{ad}\delta_{kl}^{bc}+\delta_{ij}^{cd}\delta_{kl}^{ab}-\delta_{ij}^{bd}\delta_{kl}^{ac}+\delta_{ij}^{bc}\delta_{kl}^{ad}.

(241)

Equivalently, introducing the six basis vectors

u_{1}=E_{ab}\,,\qquad u_{2}=E_{ac}\,,\qquad u_{3}=E_{ad}\,,\qquad u_{4}=E_{bc}\,,\qquad u_{5}=E_{bd}\,,\qquad u_{6}=E_{cd}\,,

(242)

one has

O=u_{1}u_{6}^{\top}+u_{6}u_{1}^{\top}-u_{2}u_{5}^{\top}-u_{5}u_{2}^{\top}+u_{3}u_{4}^{\top}+u_{4}u_{3}^{\top}.

(243)

Here, the
E_{ij}
are orthonormal with respect to the standard inner product on 2-forms induced by the standard inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, namely

\langle u\wedge v,\;x\wedge y\rangle=\langle u,x\rangle\langle v,y\rangle-\langle u,y\rangle\langle v,x\rangle\,.

(244)

Now, since
u_{1},\dots,u_{6}
are orthonormal, we can directly compute

\mathbb{O}=O^{2}=(u_{1}u_{6}^{\top}+u_{6}u_{1}^{\top})^{2}+(-u_{2}u_{5}^{\top}-u_{5}u_{2}^{\top})^{2}+(u_{3}u_{4}^{\top}+u_{4}u_{3}^{\top})^{2}\,.

(245)

Each square is the projector onto its
2
-dimensional support

\displaystyle(u_{1}u_{6}^{\top}+u_{6}u_{1}^{\top})^{2}

\displaystyle=u_{1}u_{1}^{\top}+u_{6}u_{6}^{\top}\,,

(246)

\displaystyle(-u_{2}u_{5}^{\top}-u_{5}u_{2}^{\top})^{2}

\displaystyle=u_{2}u_{2}^{\top}+u_{5}u_{5}^{\top}\,,

(247)

\displaystyle(u_{3}u_{4}^{\top}+u_{4}u_{3}^{\top})^{2}

\displaystyle=u_{3}u_{3}^{\top}+u_{4}u_{4}^{\top}\,.

(248)

Namely

\mathbb{O}=\sum_{r=1}^{6}u_{r}u_{r}^{\top}\,.

(249)

Thus,
\mathbb{O}
is the orthogonal projector onto the
6
-dimensional subspace spanned by
E_{ab},E_{ac},E_{ad},E_{bc},E_{bd},E_{cd}
. This readily implies that its spectrum is

\operatorname{spec}(\mathbb{O})=\{1\}^{[6]}\cup\{0\}^{[\binom{2n}{2}-6]}.

(250)

Hence

\|\mathbb{O}\|_{1}=\operatorname{Tr}(\mathbb{O})=6,\qquad\|\mathbb{O}\|_{2}^{2}=\operatorname{Tr}(\mathbb{O}^{2})=6,

(251)

and so

\left(\frac{\|\mathbb{O}\|_{2}}{\|\mathbb{O}\|_{1}}\right)^{2}=\frac{6}{6^{2}}=\frac{1}{6}\,,

(252)

completing the proof.
∎

C.3.3
Pauli observables (in
\mathcal{B}_{4}
) and Gaussian states do not form GPs, but random states may help

As Theorems
C.3
and
C.4
in the previous two sections identify, neither Gaussian states nor Pauli observables in
\mathcal{B}_{4}
alone can lead to GPs. Therefore, combining both definitely fails to meet Condition
2
. This violation implies, as per Theorem
2
, that Gaussian states combined with Pauli observables in
\mathcal{B}_{4}
cannot lead to GPs. Although Theorems
C.3
and
C.4
explicitly demonstrate this violation for the univariate case, it naturally follows that multivariate GPs are likewise impossible. Because the set of multivariate moments strictly contains all univariate moments, the univariate failure inherently precludes any multivariate generalization. Supplemental Fig.
C.3
b illustrates this violation from a
12
-qubit simulation.

Theorem
C.4
implies that if the observable
O
is a Pauli operator in
\mathcal{B}_{4}
, the emergence of a GP can only be obtained through the state choice, which Gaussian states fail to achieve. Therefore, GPs from a Pauli observable in
\mathcal{B}_{4}
require strong conditions on the family of states. While analytically characterizing such states is generally challenging, we provide numerical evidence in Supp. Fig.
C.3
a. Specifically, we show numerically that a random fermionic state, i.e., a random positive eigenstate of the parity operator
Z^{\otimes n}
, could yield a univariate GP.
It is worth noting that Proposition
C.5
and the subsequent remark rigorously establish that all odd moments are zero for any single Pauli observable in
\mathcal{B}_{4}
.

Supplemental Figure C.3:
A plausible univariate GP vs a provable violation for a Pauli observable in
\mathcal{B}_{4}
.
We compare the empirical statistics of the expectation values of a random Pauli drawn from
\mathcal{B}_{4}
when: a) the input state is a random fermionic state, b) the input state is Gaussian. In both cases, the state is then evolved under a Haar-random matchgate (free-fermionic) transformation before being measured.
The system size considered in this analysis is
n=12
, and the histograms were generated from
10^{4}
samples for both panels. On top of the blue histograms, we plot the fitted Gaussian curve (in orange) obtained from the empirical mean and variance of the samples. Furthermore, in the legend we report the Kullback-Leibler divergence (KL) between the fitted Gaussian distribution and the samples’ one. Both the KL values and the visual agreement between the histogram and the fitted curve suggest that random fermionic states could yield a GP, while Gaussian states do not.

C.4
GPs from special observables in any module
\mathcal{B}_{m}

We here consider a special class of observables
O
that can be constructed in any module
\mathcal{B}_{m}
, which provably leads to moments required to obey GPs. These observables form linear combinations of
\mathcal{B}_{m}
basis operators associated with mutually disjoint Majorana indices, which is formally defined in the following. This observable is based on a (partial) partition of the
2n
Majorana operator indices into mutually disjoint index sets (of size
m
), i.e.,

\bm{\mathcal{V}}=\left\{\mathcal{V}^{(j)}\subseteq[2n]\mid j\leq 1\leq\lfloor 2n/m\rfloor,\,\lvert\mathcal{V}^{(j)}\rvert=m\right\}\quad\text{such that}\quad\forall j\neq j^{\prime}\colon\mathcal{V}^{(j)}\cap\mathcal{V}^{(j^{\prime})}=\varnothing\,.

(253)

Hence, each of these index subsets
\mathcal{V}^{(j)}
labels one module basis operator
C_{\mathcal{V}^{(j)}}
of the module
\mathcal{B}_{m}
such that these operators, which are products of
m
distinct Majorana operators, never share the same Majorana operator.
Clearly,
|\bm{\mathcal{V}}|=\lfloor 2n/m\rfloor
. Then, the partition
\bm{\mathcal{V}}
completely covers all
2n
Majorana indices if
2n
is a multiple of
m
, and, otherwise, is a partial partition, which leaves
2n\bmod m
indices uncovered.

Therefore, the form of such module partition observables
O
reads as

O=\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor 2n/m\rfloor}\omega_{j}C_{\mathcal{V}^{(j)}}\,,

(254)

where
\omega_{j}
are non-zero real coefficients that we assume to be constant
\Theta(1)
in the number of qubits
n
.
Crucially, this is in contrast to summing over all possible indices and corresponding module basis operators
C_{\mathcal{V}}
, which would lead to any element of
\mathcal{B}_{m}
.

These observables have the nice property that their associated tensors
\bm{O}
can be expressed as a sum of mutually orthogonal generalized (anti-symmetric) Kronecker deltas

o_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}}={\rm Tr}\left[\hat{O}C_{\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}}\right]=\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor 2n/m\rfloor}\omega_{j}\delta^{(j)}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}}\,.

(255)

Here, the index
j
associates the Kronecker delta with the index subset
\mathcal{V}^{(i)}
. Let
\nu_{1}<\dots<\nu_{m}
be the strictly ordered indices of
\mathcal{V}^{(j)}
, we can then define
\delta^{(j)}
via the standard notation for the generalized Kronecker delta as

\delta^{(j)}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}}=\delta^{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{m}}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}}\,,

(256)

and further defined in Eq. (
64
),
which automatically enforces total anti-symmetry and evaluates to zero unless the lower indices
\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}
are a permutation of the subset
\mathcal{V}^{(j)}
.

By the generalized Kronecker deltas being
mutually orthogonal
we mean that any possible contraction involving multiple tensors
\delta^{(j)}
vanishes identically unless every tensor shares the exact same subset index
j
. This follows directly from the fact that the subsets
\mathcal{V}^{(j)}
are mutually disjoint (
\mathcal{V}^{(j)}\cap\mathcal{V}^{(j^{\prime})}=\varnothing
for
j\neq j^{\prime}
). Because a component of
\delta^{(j)}
is non-zero only when its indices are exclusively from
\mathcal{V}^{(j)}
, any contraction between tensors with different subset indices forces a mismatch, evaluating to zero.
Consequently, when evaluating the tensor contractions required for the moments according to Eq. (
B.2
), there are no cross-terms and the contributions from each index subset
\mathcal{V}^{(j)}
decouple completely. Intuitively, if one simply orders the indices so that the elements of each subset
\mathcal{V}^{(j)}
are grouped together, the observable tensor
\bm{O}
reveals a strictly block-diagonal structure consisting of isolated blocks of size
m
.

With these useful properties established, we now study the odd and even moments.

We prove that for the class of observables here considered, the odd moments always vanish, non-trivially because any possible contraction of an odd number
k
of module frame coefficient tensors
\bm{O}
vanishes:

Proposition C.5

(Odd moments for special observable)
.

For the special case of observables
O
defined in Eq. (
254
),
all odd moments vanish.

Proof.

As per Lemma
B.2
, the odd moments trivially vanish for odd
m
(also cf. Lemma
B.1
), and, hence, the main part of the proof focuses on modules
\mathcal{B}_{m}
with even
m
and shows that vanishing odd
k
moments, expressed via contractions determined by the Brauer algebra
\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}
.

Recall that if two tensors
\delta^{(j)}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}},\delta^{(j^{\prime})}_{\beta_{1}\dots\beta_{m}},
with
j\neq j^{\prime}
are involved in a contraction the latter will vanish. Hence we can assume without loss of generality that
o_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}}=\omega_{j}\delta^{(j)}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}}
for one choice of index
j
and, hence, index subset
\mathcal{V}_{j}
.
Now, effectively the indices
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}
can be restricted to the
m
possible values
\{\nu_{1},\dots,\nu_{m}\}
, since any other value in
[2n]
yields zero.
Hence, we recover arbitrary contractions of
k
copies of
\delta_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}}^{\nu_{1}\dots\nu_{m}}
, which corresponds to matching
mk/2
indices and summing over all their possible values.
However, the complete anti-symmetry of these tensors implies that only the combinations where the indices of every single tensor take different values survive.
We can think of this as an instance of an edge-coloring graph problem, for an
m
-regular multigraph of
k
vertices/nodes. Indeed, the
m
possible values that the indices
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}
can take can be associated with
m
possible colors, while the edges of the multigraph are provided by the contraction at hand, i.e., the Brauer algebra element, and the vertices are the tensors.

We highlight that if it were possible to edge-color an
m
-regular graph with exactly
m
colors, each color would have to visit every vertex exactly once. In terms of the tensors, this means any specific index value (e.g.,
\nu_{1}
) must appear exactly once within each of the
k
copies of
\delta^{(j)}
.
Consequently, this specific index value must appear a total of
k
times across the entire expression. However, every contraction inherently links exactly two indices together. Since
k
is odd, it is algebraically impossible to group
k
occurrences into pairs. This contradiction dictates that no valid index assignment can simultaneously satisfy the contraction lines and distinct index values for the Kronecker delta tensors, meaning every term evaluates to zero, completing the proof.
∎

Notice that for a general observable
O
, the graph coloring argument for its tensor
\bm{O}
corresponds to
2n
available colors rather than
m
. This implies that the vanishing of odd moments cannot be deduced directly from anti-symmetry properties alone, but must instead stem from explicit cancellations between the tensor components.

Furthermore, note that the proof for the vanishing of odd moments relies neither on the non-zero requirement of the weights in Eq. (
254
), nor on the large-
n
limit or any associated scaling arguments. Instead, it strictly requires only that the observable is a linear combination of basis operators
C_{\mathcal{V}^{(j)}}
supported on mutually disjoint Majorana index subsets
\mathcal{V}^{(j)}\subseteq[2n]
.

All that is left to show is that the even moments coming from this class of observables converge to those of a Gaussian in the large-
n
limit, that is, that Condition
2
is satisfied.

Proposition C.6

(Even moments for special observable)
.

For the special class of observables
O
defined in Eq. (
254
)

\frac{\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}\!\left(\bm{O}^{\otimes k}\right)}{\lVert O\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}^{k}}\in\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\,,

(257)

for any even
k
and contraction
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}
that does not correspond to pairwise matchings of
k
tensor copies
\bm{O}
.

Proof.

Let us start by computing the result of a full contraction between two copies of the tensor
\bm{O}
governed by an arbitrary index permutation
\pi\in S_{m}
, denoted
\operatorname{contr}_{\pi}\!\left(\bm{O}^{\otimes 2}\right)
. This reads

\operatorname{contr}_{\pi}\!\left(\bm{O}^{\otimes 2}\right)=\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}\omega_{j}\omega_{j^{\prime}}\delta^{(j)}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}}\delta^{(j^{\prime})}_{\alpha_{\pi(1)}\dots\alpha_{\pi(m)}}\,,

(258)

where Einstein summation over the indices
\{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}\}
is intended. The magnitude of these contractions arises as factors
\lVert O\rVert_{\mathcal{B}_{m}}^{2}
in the denominator in Eq. (
257
). Using the mutual orthogonality of the anti-symmetric Kronecker delta tensors for
j\neq j^{\prime}
, and their total anti-symmetry which yields
\delta^{(j)}_{\alpha_{\pi(1)}\dots\alpha_{\pi(m)}}=\operatorname{sgn}(\pi)\delta^{(j)}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}}
, we find

\operatorname{contr}_{\pi}\!\left(\bm{O}^{\otimes 2}\right)=\operatorname{sgn}(\pi)\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor 2n/m\rfloor}\omega_{j}^{2}\delta^{(j)}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}}\delta^{(j)}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}}=\operatorname{sgn}(\pi)m!\sum_{j=1}^{\lfloor 2n/m\rfloor}\omega_{j}^{2}\in\Theta(n)\,,

(259)

where we used the unpermuted full contraction
\delta^{(j)}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}}\delta^{(j)}_{\alpha_{1}\dots\alpha_{m}}=m!
,
and the fact that the weights are asymptotically bounded as
\omega_{j}\in\Theta(1)
, ensuring the sum scales within linear bounds with qubit number
n
.
Consequently, the denominator in Eq. (
257
), corresponding to the desired contractions in the even
k
moments achieved via pairwise tensor matchings, implies that each of the
k/2
tensor pairs yields a factor
\lVert O\rVert_{m}^{2}=\lvert\operatorname{contr}_{\pi}(\bm{O}^{\otimes 2})\rvert
for any
\pi\in S_{m}
, resulting in an overall denominator scaling of
\Theta(n^{k/2})
.

Now, the spurious contractions
\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}(\bm{O}^{\otimes k})
with
\sigma\in\mathfrak{B}_{mk/2}
restricted to a non-pairwise tensor matching necessarily involve tensor matchings of three or more copies of
\bm{O}
. While the previously proven Proposition
C.5
allows us to immediately discard any contraction involving an odd number of copies, we still need to address those contractions matching an even number, greater than 2, of them.
However, whenever such a non-pairwise contraction arises and does not vanish, the mutual orthogonality of the generalized Kronecker deltas forces that any two contracted tensors must share the exact same subset index
j
. By transitivity, every tensor within a fully connected component is locked to the identical subset index. This rigidly collapses their independent multiple index summations into a single, shared summation over the index subsets in
\bm{\mathcal{V}}
. Because neither the weights
\omega_{j}
nor the generalized Kronecker delta tensors carry any dependence on
n
(yielding only
\Theta(1)
combinatorial factors in
m
), this single summation over the index subsets in
\bm{\mathcal{V}}
evaluates strictly to
\Theta(n)
.
We thus understand that any generalized cycle
11
11

11

We use the term
generalized cycle
to denote a fully connected, inseparable component of tensors within the overall contraction
\sigma
, which, in contrast to the case of tensors with rank
m=2
(matrices), no longer form visual cycles in a tensor network diagram.

\mathcal{C}
, no matter its length
|\mathcal{C}|
, between the
k
copies of tensor
\bm{O}
contributes a factor of
\Theta(n)
to the total contraction
\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}(\bm{O}^{\otimes k})
which hence scales as

\operatorname{contr}_{\sigma}\left(\bm{O}^{\otimes k}\right)=\prod_{\mathcal{C}\in\operatorname{cycles}(\sigma)}\Theta(n)=\Theta\left(n^{\lvert\operatorname{cycles}(\sigma)\rvert}\right)\,,

(260)

where we defined
\operatorname{cycles}(\sigma)
as the generalized cycles of
\sigma
and, hence,
\lvert\operatorname{cycles}(\sigma)\rvert
as the number of such cycles.
Since this is exactly maximized by the contractions corresponding to the pairings, i.e., cycles of length 2, it follows that the spurious contractions vanish at least as
\mathcal{O}(1/n)
relative to the pairwise denominator
\lvert\operatorname{contr}_{\pi}(\bm{O}^{\otimes 2})\rvert^{k/2}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Given the vanishing of the odd moments and the partial fulfillment of Condition
2
for the even moments, this proves that for this special class of observables defined in Eq. (
254
), GPs emerge via Theorem
1
.
Generally, the complete fulfillment of Condition
2
is subject to an appropriate scaling behavior of the states, which can be checked equivalently via Proposition
B.2
in the univariate case, or sufficiently via Proposition
B.3
(or directly by probing Condition
2
or Proposition
B.4
).

Furthermore, we note that the partitioning requirement can be relaxed such that the subsets do not need to cover all
2n
indices. As long as the subsets
\mathcal{V}^{(j)}
remain mutually disjoint, which guarantees the exact vanishing of the odd moments, and the total number of partitions
|\bm{\mathcal{V}}|
scales linearly with
n
(i.e.,
|\bm{\mathcal{V}}|=\Theta(n)
), the asymptotic domination of the Gaussian pairings in the even moments established in Proposition
C.6
remains entirely valid. For instance, single Pauli observables in
\mathcal{B}_{m}
do not meet such
\lvert\bm{\mathcal{V}}\rvert
scaling. While the odd moments vanish since Proposition
C.5
even applies to this relaxed observable form, such single Pauli observables do not suffice to achieve the required even-moment scaling (Proposition
C.6
does not apply), e.g., consistent with Proposition
C.4
for
\mathcal{B}_{4}
.

Supp. Info. D
Efficient measurement protocol for matchgate kernel estimation

We introduce a measurement protocol for the matchgate quantum kernel functions
\kappa(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
that avoids an indirect and naive estimation of the module projection coefficients (Eq. (
60
)) for both states
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
and classically computing the kernel value based on them according to the module overlap definition in Eq. (
68
). Instead, a direct estimation by simultaneously preparing
\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime}
, gathering Bell basis measurement statistics, and classical (efficient) post-processing provides kernel estimates.
The relevant quantity that has to be estimated from the quantum input states
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
is the module overlap
\left\langle\rho,\rho^{\prime}\right\rangle_{m}
, while the remaining factors
{m!}/{(2n)^{m}}\lVert{{O}}\rVert^{2}_{m}
are known exactly classically. Hence, we denote the matchgate kernel function explicitly as a function of
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
as
\kappa(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
here, as opposed to a function of state labels
t,t^{\prime}
.

It is more convenient to consider an equivalent form, introducing the natural scaling factor
d=2^{n}
, as

\kappa(\rho,\rho^{\prime})=\frac{m!}{(2n)^{m}}\left(d^{-1}\lVert{{O}}\rVert^{2}_{m}\right)\left(d\langle\rho,\rho^{\prime}\rangle_{m}\right).

(261)

We can think of these rescaled module overlaps and norms as the effective module quantities, which typically arise as follows.
The (squared) effective module norm
d^{-1}\lVert{{O}}\rVert^{2}_{m}
, is computed classically, and typically corresponds to a quantity that, even after dividing by
d
, does not vanish exponentially with
n
for typical observables
O
, such as any Pauli strings in
\mathcal{B}_{m}
. Furthermore, when expressing
O
directly as a linear combination of distinct Majorana products (or their corresponding Pauli strings) in
\mathcal{B}_{m}
, i.e., without the normalization factor in Eq. (
58
), the corresponding sum of squared coefficients yields
d^{-1}\lVert{{O}}\rVert^{2}_{m}
. The second term,
d\langle\rho,\rho^{\prime}\rangle_{m}
resembles an effectively measured variant of the module overlaps, whose estimation will be discussed in detail below.

D.1
Direct matchgate kernel estimation

The direct estimation protocol for a matchgate kernel value
\kappa(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
consists of the following steps, which are detailed subsequently:

1.

Prepare states
\rho
and
\rho^{\prime}
coherently

2.

Perform measurements in the Bell basis

3.

Classically compute (in polynomial time) an unbiased estimate of
d\Braket{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}_{m}
and, hence,
\kappa(\rho,\rho^{\prime})
via Eq. (
261
)

The module overlap can be written as

\Braket{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}_{m}={\rm Tr}\left[(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime})\sum_{\mathcal{V}}C_{\mathcal{V}}^{\dagger}\otimes C_{\mathcal{V}}\right]\,,

(262)

where
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,2n\}
with
\lvert\mathcal{V}\rvert=m
denotes all subsets of
\{1,\ldots,2n\}
of cardinality
m
, i.e.,
\{C_{\mathcal{V}}\}_{\mathcal{V}}
forms a basis for module
\mathcal{B}_{m}
as defined in Eq. (
58
).
Analogously, the rescaled variant, which is the quantity to be measured, reads as

d\Braket{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}_{m}={\rm Tr}\left[(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime})\sum_{\mathcal{V}}\sqrt{d}C_{\mathcal{V}}^{\dagger}\otimes\sqrt{d}C_{\mathcal{V}}\right]={\rm Tr}\left[(\rho\otimes\rho^{\prime})\sum_{\mathcal{V}}P_{\mathcal{V}}\otimes P_{\mathcal{V}}\right],

(263)

where we denote
P_{\mathcal{V}}
as the Majorana product that corresponds to the module basis operator
C_{\mathcal{V}}
without the normalization factor
12
12

12

The factor
h_{m}
in Eq. (
58
) cancels out due to its unit modulus.
. Hence,
P_{\mathcal{V}}
is a Pauli string.
For any states
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
, the effective module overlaps can be bounded
13
13

13

Note that for certain modules, bounds are known to be tighter than the module dimension
d_{m}
, such as
n
in the
\mathcal{B}_{2}
case
Deneris et al. 2025
.
as

\left\lvert d\Braket{\rho,\rho^{\prime}}_{m}\right\rvert\leq d_{m}\quad\text{with}\quad d_{m}=\dim(\mathcal{B}_{m})=\binom{2n}{m}.

(264)

The Bell basis
\{\ket{P}\}_{P\in\mathcal{P}}
, with
P
an
n
-qubit Pauli string and
\mathcal{P}=\{I,X,Y,Z\}^{n}
denoting the set of all
4^{n}
such strings, is defined as

\ket{P}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}P\otimes I_{n}\ket{\Omega}

(265)

with
\ket{\Omega}
denoting the un-normalized maximally entangled state over two
n
-qubit systems
\ket{\Omega}=\sum_{i=1}^{2^{n}}\ket{i}\otimes\ket{i}
.
An important property is that all terms in the operator sums in Eqs. (
262
) and (
263
) diagonalize in the Bell basis:

Proposition D.1
.

For any module basis operator
C_{\mathcal{V}}
, the tensor product
C_{\mathcal{V}}^{\dagger}\otimes C_{\mathcal{V}}
is diagonal in the Bell basis
\{\ket{P}\}_{P\in\mathcal{P}}
.

Proof.

Via Jordan-Wigner mapping and module basis definition in Eq. (
58
), any tensor product of a product of
m
Majorana operators and its adjoint
\sqrt{d}C_{\mathcal{V}}^{\dagger}\otimes\sqrt{d}C_{\mathcal{V}}=C_{n_{1}}C_{n_{2}}\cdots C_{n_{m}}\otimes C_{n_{1}}C_{n_{2}}\cdots C_{n_{m}}
is represented by
P\otimes P
with
P\in\mathcal{P}
.
Note that
I,X,Z
are symmetric while
Y
is anti-symmetric, i.e.,
I^{\top}=I,X^{\top}=X,Y^{\top}=-Y,Z^{\top}=Z
and that any Pauli strings
P,Q
either commute or anti-commute, leading to either
PQ=QP
or
PQ=-QP
. As a special case of the latter, Pauli conjugation preserves the Pauli as either
PQP=Q
or
PQP=-Q
.
Together with the identity
A\otimes I\ket{\Omega}=I\otimes A^{\top}\ket{\Omega}

Nielsen and Chuang 2000
it follows that

\displaystyle P\otimes P\ket{Q}

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{d}}(P\otimes P)(Q\otimes I)\ket{\Omega}

(266)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{d}}(PQ\otimes I)(I\otimes P)\ket{\Omega}

(267)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{d}}PQP^{\top}\otimes I\ket{\Omega}

(268)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\varphi PQP\otimes I\ket{\Omega}

(269)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\varphi\varphi^{\prime}Q\otimes I\ket{\Omega}

(270)

\displaystyle=\varphi\varphi^{\prime}\ket{Q}

(271)

for any (
n
-qubit) Pauli strings
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}
, where
\varphi=\pm 1
and
